#3006. Winners & Sinners: Week 3

Welcome to Winners & Sinners, where we reflect on the week that was for the Milwaukee Bucks. Week 3 saw the Bucks go 0-3, with the team losing to the Minnesota Timberwolves, Boston Celtics, and Toronto Raptors, despite some valiant efforts that put them in positions to win. But who's deserving of the hardware?

Prior to the Raptors game, this was a legitimate conversation between Myles Turner and Tyler Herro. Then Turner pulled one of his all-too-common vanishing acts. Meanwhile, Herro put in his best playmaking performance as a Buck to make it a no contest. And although he's struggled in first quarters—zero first-quarter points this week—it hasn't really mattered. He heats up. Quickly. But as Nate O. said in our staff thread, "he's definitely more than just a microwave—he's an air fryer."

And so the nickname was born: Tyler Herro, the Air Fryer. Please make this a thing.

The Contenders: Myles Turner, Ryan Rollins.

The Decision: I was so ready to anoint Turner as the defensive champion following his showings against the Timberwolves and Celtics, where he was a force blocking shots, accumulating seven across the two games, and also doing yeoman's work on the boards (at least for him). Then he made it look like the Bucks would have to give up picks in any swap for Jakob Poeltl. Rollins had his moments too, especially when hounding Derrick White. But this belt ain't no participation award; folks need to take it. And so it's still collecting dust in the locker room.

This week's champion is Sausagemahoney, who raised a very thought-provoking question about the Bucks' decision to take Brayden Burries over Yaxel Lendeborg, who's since given the zombie Golden State Warriors new life—and who legend Chris Mullin thinks is "going to be a perennial All-Star and just an All-NBA type":

Truthfully, I'm just jealous I didn't pose the question for this week's Bucks Reacts Survey.

If, prior to the season, someone told you that Nate Ament would play fourth-quarter minutes for the Bucks—not the Herd—in the third week of the season, you'd have assumed it would come in garbage time. If they told you he would be a key contributor in a 22-point comeback, splashing an above-the-break three-pointer to cut the lead to two, you'd have called them crazy. Yeah, the Bucks didn't go on to win this one. And, yes, the Bucks are in the trenches. But moments like this give you a reason to believe.

No hesitation, no rim. No ceiling. Not for this rookie.

Whoever coined "Kuzmania" absolutely nailed it—even if it was originally intended as a positive. Like "mania's" psychiatric denotation, Kyle Kuzma's game is just all over the place far too often for a tenth-year veteran. This week's case in point came at the 2:59 mark of the fourth quarter against the Boston Celtics when, after coming back from a 22-point deficit and down only two, Kuzma decides to take a corner three with 19 seconds left on the shot-clock. 19! It wasn't even as if he was hot—he'd hit only two field goals all game. Of course, after it clanks off rim, the Celtics go on to collect multiple offensive rebounds on the next possession, before Derrick White makes an and-one layup to extend the lead to five. Game over. But the most infuriating thing isn't even that it cost the Bucks the win, it's that Kuzma is supposed to be a veteran presence teaching the Bucks' youth. Some lesson.

If Taylor Jenkins moving Brayden Burries into the starting lineup is a signpost of his willingness to tinker, then he has to do so elsewhere too. There is absolutely no reason to continue to start Kuzma and play him heavy minutes. None!

That's all for Week 3. Share your winners and sinners in the comments!

#2998. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

#2993. Nets vs. Hawks preview: Brooklyn opens four-game road trip in Atlanta

ATLANTA, GA – NOVEMBER 6: Kingston Flemings #4 of the Atlanta Hawks handles the ball during the game against the Washington Wizards during the 2026 Emirates NBA Cup on November 6, 2026 at State Farm Arena in Atlanta, Georgia. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and/or using this Photograph, user is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. Mandatory Copyright Notice: Copyright 2026 NBAE (Photo by Adam Hagy/NBAE via Getty Images) | NBAE via Getty Images

Hey, a win! The Brooklyn Nets entered Wednesday night's game against the Charlotte Hornets searching for their first home win of the season. Thankfully for them, it finally came thanks to a big fourth quarter run as Brooklyn came away with a 114-104 victory.

Now, Brooklyn takes that momentum on the road, beginning a four-game trip against an Atlanta team coming off an offensive explosion. The Atlanta Hawks are hoping to go far in the postseason this year and position themselves for a promising future. They helped the cause with a 139-132 victory over the Washington Wizards on Friday night.

Matchup: Brooklyn Nets (2-6) vs. Atlanta Hawks (6-4)

Watch: YES Network, DAZN, NBA League Pass

Questionable – Drake Powell (G-League assignment)

OUT – Mouhamed Gueye (left foot fracture)

OUT – Keshon Gilbert (left knee inflammation)

OUT – Asa Newell (left great toe hyperextension)

This night one of a back-to-back for Brooklyn. We'll see if anything changes before tip-off.

The Nets are on the first night of a back-to-back and first game of a four game road trip. They'll head to Dallas for a Sunday evening matchup with the Mavericks.

The Hawks are on the second night of a home back-to-back. They wrap up their brief three game homestand on Monday night against the Los Angeles Lakers. They hope to do something big this spring, but they have their doubters. From Ricky O'Donnell at the mothership:

"This team doesn't have enough force going to the basket, and the defense isn't anything special despite generating a lot of takeaways and forcing misses from three. The Hawks look mid as always."

It takes time to get used to someone in a new role. We've spent so much time with Nickeil Alexander-Walker being a solid bench role player that it's still somewhat surprising to see him operate as a high(er) usage wing as a starter. Alexander-Walker won the Most Improved Player award last year and he's carried that success over to this year. He's averaging a career best 22.3 points per game, and most impressively is getting to the line over four times a game. When Johnson returns, we'll see how the Hawks look to climb the standings.

Nolan Traore keeps stacking good days together. The second year guard had another good game as a starter on Wednesday night and he's making a great case to remain the starter once Egor returns. Traore can get to the rim and is second on the team in drives per game.

We'll get to watch a rookie who finally made his way into the starting lineup.

CJ McCollum requested that he come off the bench and the kid gets to start. With the Hawks looking to establish their future, a selfless decision like that from McCollum will go a long way in establishing Flemings as a key player going forward. For Atlanta, finding out what they have in Flemings will go a long way in determining their future. He's played over 23 minutes in four out of the last five games and with this being night two of the b2b, we'll see how long Quin Snyder plays him for.

Mikel Brown Jr is not on the injury report, so he'll play tonight and will more than likely sit tomorrow night in Dallas. The Nets are continuing to build Brown Jr and trust that his growth will lead to an exciting future. The drives are there, but if the three pointers start to become consistent, the sky is the limit for him and the Nets. It's all about making the most of the opportunities that are available to you.

Last weekend, the Atlanta Dream fell short in Game 7 of the WNBA Finals against the Golden State Valkyries. The last do-or-die game to decide the W Finals was a pretty celebratory occasion around these parts.

#2990. Patriots activate Mack Hollins from injured reserve on eve of Week 9

Mack Hollins will get the green light against the Green Bay Packers.

The New England Patriots officially activated the veteran wide receiver on the eve of Sunday's game at Gillette Stadium.

A calf injury had sent Hollins to injured reserve a week into October. After missing the NFL minimum of four games, he saw his 21-day practice window open as the calendar turned to November. Three limited sessions and a questionable game designation followed.

Hollins, 33, now returns as the eldest statesman on an active depth chart of four. With A.J. Brown ruled out for a second consecutive matchup due to a knee injury and Romeo Doubs still residing on injured reserve due to a shoulder injury, DeMario Douglas, Kyle Williams and Efton Chism III will make up the rest of the wideout room for the 4:25 p.m. ET kickoff.

At the time of his non-contact exit last month at Highmark Stadium, Hollins stood atop the offense with 20 targets, 15 catches and 209 yards through four appearances. The 6-foot-4, 221-pound North Carolina product finds himself 131 games, 56 starts, 223 catches, 2,828 yards and 17 touchdowns into a career that began with the Philadelphia Eagles in the fourth round of the 2017 NFL draft at No. 118 overall.

A Super Bowl LII champion, Hollins made subsequent stops with the Miami Dolphins, Las Vegas Raiders, Atlanta Falcons and Buffalo Bills before arriving in Foxborough on a two-year deal in 2025.

New England carried an open spot on the 53-man roster after releasing and retaining veteran cornerback Kindle Vildor on the practice squad ahead of the Green Bay visit. No standard elevations were made for Sunday afternoon.

Tight end Hunter Henry, offensive tackle Morgan Moses, cornerback Carlton Davis III and safeties Kevin Byard III and Brenden Schooler move forward with questionable game statuses, while guard Greg Van Roten will remain on injured reserve after also having his practice window open at the start of the week.

#2985. Federal Reserve Board releases results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2984. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#2977. Highlights: Spurs handle Kings behind blazing three-point shooting

Coming off a thrilling win against the Dallas Mavericks, the Spurs traveled to Sacramento to take on the Kings in their first NBA Cup game of the season. From the opening tip, the Spurs' offense showed no rust from Wednesday. They outscored the Kings 38-29 in the first quarter and took a 19-point lead into halftime. Despite a low-scoring third quarter, the lead remained the same as the Kings' defense had no answer for the Spurs' hot shooting from three-point range. They shot 50% (15-30) after shooting an abysmal 15% (5-36) against Dallas. The Spurs ultimately won 124-109, and extended their winning streak to seven.

Victor Wembanyama followed up his career-best performance of 52 points, 24 rebounds, and 8 blocks with 30 points (10-for-18 FG, 4-for-6 3PT, 6-for-9 FT), 14 rebounds, 2 blocks, and 2 assists. Despite Wemby's Wilt Chamberlain-esque performance this past Wednesday, his three-point shooting was forgettable (1-8 3PT). Wemby quieted the critics who have wanted him to stop shooting threes by shooting a scorching 66% on four-for-six against the Kings.

He continued to control the glass on both ends and demonstrated that no one can guard him in the paint, especially on lob attempts and putbacks.

Stephon Castle, Dylan Harper, and Devin Vassell each dropped 19 points to balance out the Spurs' offensive attack. All three of them shot efficiently from the field: Castle shot 7 for 13 (54%), Harper shot 5 for 10 (50%), and Vassell shot 6 for 10 (60%), with five for six from three-point range (83%). It was a great sign to see Devin light the nets on fire after going almost scoreless against Dallas. His ability to spread the floor and get to his spots helps the three guards penetrate and kick out to him for an easy three-point laser. In some cases, his range might surprise you.

Dylan continues to be one of the best finishers in the league, and he got most of his buckets with ease against a Kings team with not a lot of defensive resistance. In transition, Dylan made rookie Darius Acuff Jr. look silly with a eurostep into a layup for two.

HIGHLIGHT OF THE NIGHT: Steph not only got his fair share of tough finishes and dimes (seven assists), but he put Dylan Cardwell on a poster. Cardwell had just scored on the other end and celebrated by wiggling his tongue out and slapping the floor in a defensive stance. Wemby and Steph both decided to humble with an AREA 51 lob but with Wemby as the passer. And one!

Here's another angle of the staredown Steph gives Cardwell afterwards.

As stated earlier, the silver and black shot uber-efficiently from three, including down the stretch to close the game out. Harrison Barnes even chipped in 13 points with a pair of threes. This is when the team's offense is at its best. Swinging the rock around and punishing the defense with open threes and great looks inside.

Finally, here are the full game highlights.

The Spurs face a SEGABABA against the Portland Trail Blazers in the Moda Center later tonight at 9:00 P.M. (CST) on DAZN.

#2972. Federal Reserve issues FOMC statement

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2970. NFL news, live updates: Rams' Myles Garrett in line to return Sunday; Shedeur Sanders to start for Browns with Deshaun Watson in concussion protocol

We're officially into Week 9 of the NFL season (and the looming NFL trade deadline), where teams will have to make a decision on the intended course of the remainder of their season — are they going all in? Or is it time to tear it all down and look toward the future?

NFL fans also await news on the fate of star running back Jonathan Taylor, who has been touted as a potential trade-deadline mover as the Indianapolis Colts kicked off their franchise reset with a fire sale. They've already traded away two foundational players, Quenton Nelson and DeForest Buckner, for several draft picks as they look to the future, leaving us to wonder whether Taylor could be next.

The Los Angeles Rams could be a deadline buyer, as they've won back-to-back games and are already getting a long-awaited midseason reinforcement in the form of edge rusher Myles Garrett, a two-time NFL Defensive Player of the Year and the league's single-season sack record holder.

Garrett's in line for a return to game action on the road Sunday against the Washington Commanders. Coming back from early-season knee surgery that landed him on injured reserve, Garrett has practiced all week.

Shedeur Sanders will start at quarterback for the Cleveland Browns in Week 9 against the New Orleans Saints, head coach Todd Monken announced.

Deshaun Watson returned to practice in a limited capacity on Friday, but remains in concussion protocol. Monken said it's unclear if Watson will serve as the backup this week, though he will be listed as questionable

The Browns' No. 1 quarterback took a helmet-to-helmet hit during the first quarter of Sunday's loss to the Pittsburgh Steelers. Watson missed only four snaps, but later reported experiencing concussion symptoms on Monday night.

Follow along with Yahoo Sports for the latest NFL news, injury updates and more ahead of Week 9.

Just when it seemed the 49ers were getting a little healthier at wide receiver, the injury bug popped back up.

San Francisco activated veteran wide receiver Demarcus Robinson from injured reserve, but replaced him with WR Christian Kirk.

Robinson had a high ankle sprain in the 49ers' Week 2 game against the Dolphins. He was listed as questionable heading into the weekend, but with his activation, that game status was cleared, and he is good to go for the team's game against the Raiders.

San Francisco needed his activation in a bad way. The 49ers had ruled out Mike Evans and Deebo Samuel heading into the weekend, and then added Kirk to IR after he suffered a knee injury at Thursday's practice.

Robinson will make his return and immediately become the WR1 for the offense along with Jordan Watkins, Brandin Cooks and Jacob Cowing.

The Broncos are getting a reinforcement in the passing game.

Denver has activated wide receiver Pat Bryant from injured reserve. Bryant played in the team's first four games before suffering an injury that landed him on IR.

The wideout caught nine of his 12 targets for 133 yards and a touchdown. Bryant is expected to make his return on Sunday against the Panthers.

The Lions will be without linebacker Jack Campbell for at least the team's next four games, as they are adding him to injured reserve.

Campbell was involved in a multi-player tackle in Detroit's game against the Vikings that resulted in him leaving the game on a backboard and being taken to a local hospital.

The Lions haven't given many updates, and the most recent was from Campbell himself, who on Wednesday said that he was still in the hospital recovering. The linebacker said he has "full movement" in his lower body and is making progress on movement in his upper body.

Neither Detroit nor Campbell have given any sort of timeline for when he could return to the field. By being added to IR, Campbell would be first eligible to play again in Week 13, depending on his recovery.

Not only will the New Orleans Saints reportedly welcome rookie wide receiver Jordyn Tyson into the fold Sunday against the Cleveland Browns, but they also plan on activating left tackle Kelvin Banks Jr., per ESPN's Jeremy Fowler.

Banks, last year's No. 9 overall pick, suffered a high ankle sprain in September that required surgery. He hasn't played since Week 2.

The delayed debut of New Orleans Saints rookie wide receiver Jordyn Tyson is reportedly right around the corner.

According to Jordan Schultz, Tyson will be activated off IR to face the Browns in Week 9. The No. 8 overall pick in the 2026 NFL draft has yet to play due to a hamstring injury.

Shedeur Sanders has already been named as the starter for Week 9. The question now is if Watson, who officially cleared concussion protocol on Saturday, will serve as the backup. If he is unable to, Dillon Gabriel will be QB2.

The Denver Broncos are looking to add a tight end before Tuesday's trade deadline.

Evan Engram has 13 receptions for 136 yards and 1 touchdown, and the team reportedly wants to upgrade.

The Broncos aimed high, checking in on Arizona Cardinals All-Pro Trey McBride, who currently leads the NFL with 66 catches. Understandably, the Cardinals said no, according to ESPN. McBride was signed to a four-year extension last year that made him the highest-paid tight end in the league at the time.

Other tight ends that could be available may include the Philadelphia Eagles' Dallas Goedert, Noah Fant from the New Orleans Saints, the Atlanta Falcons' Austin Hooper and the Dallas Cowboys' Luke Schoonmaker.

According to Adam Schefter, Henry is expected to remain with the Ravens through Tuesday's NFL trade deadline.

Following Baltimore's Thursday night loss to Jacksonville, Henry reiterated that he wants to stay with the Ravens.

"I have not mentioned me going anywhere else," he said. "I'm saying, 'Let's go back to work. Let's try to get better and turn this thing around.'"

The Colts remain interested in moving pieces out as they rebuild. According to Ian Rapoport and Adam Schefter, along with discussions involving running back Jonathan Taylor, the team is listening in on suitors for quarterback Daniel Jones.

The Ravens were reportedly a team that call, but that was before they went and acquired Jacoby Brissett this week from the Cardinals.

Jones has a $10.51 million injury guarantee for this season that is fully guaranteed in March 15 if he's still on the Colts' roster. Jones could waive the injury guarantee if he wanted to move on.

The Seattle Seahawks sent a pair of draft picks to the Indianapolis Colts in exchange for six-time All-Pro guard Quenton Nelson.

After just one game in a Seahawks uniform, Nelson is now out.

He's "week-to-week," according to Seattle head coach Mike Macdonald, who told reporters Friday that the 30-year-old offensive lineman needs a minor knee procedure.

"We're not going to put him on IR," Macdonald said, via The Seattle Times' Bob Condotta.

When asked what happened to Nelson, Macdonald said the interior o-lineman had something he was dealing with when he arrived.

"We re-imaged it, and it was aggravated to some degree, so we've kind of done the due diligence, and everybody agrees it's best to take care of it now and play the long game."

Notably, the Seahawks will also be down quarterback Sam Darnold (quad) this week against the Arizona Cardinals. Backup Drew Lock will start in his place.

Detroit Lions safety Kerby Joseph is out for Week 9's matchup with the Miami Dolphins. However, Joseph took first-team reps as a limited participant in practice all week and "looked awesome," according to Lions safeties coach Jim O'Neil.

"The focus right now is let's see if we can stack some days," O'Neil told the Detroit Free Press' Dave Birkett. "Let's see if he can look like he looked yesterday for three or four days in a row."

Joseph, who turns 26 on Nov. 14, hasn't played for the Lions since Week 6 of last season due to a cartilage condition in his knee. He was an All-Pro selection in 2024, his third NFL season, compiling 9 interceptions and 83 tackles.

The Lions signed Joseph to a four-year extension in April 2025 that made him the highest-paid safety in the NFL at the time.

Injuries continue to plague the Eagles, who will again be without top receivers DeVonta Smith and Dontayvion Wicks this week against the Giants. The team has officially ruled out both wideouts for Sunday's game, after neither practiced on Friday.

Both Smith, who has been dealing with a hamstring injury, and Wicks, who has an ankle injury, have missed two games with the injury, and are set to now miss a third.

However, there's a bit of good news: Saquon Barkley, who was listed as limited in practice with hamstring and back injuries, should be clear to play on Sunday.

Kansas City Chiefs head coach Andy Reid told reporters Friday that rookies Peter Woods and Mansoor Delane won't play Sunday against the New York Jets.

"They're making progress, so that's a positive thing," Reid said of the defensive tackle and cornerback, per ESPN's Nate Taylor.

Woods is dealing with a neck injury, and Delane is navigating a shoulder issue. Delane, this year's No. 6 overall pick, hasn't played since a Week 7 loss to the Seattle Seahawks. Woods, whom the Chiefs selected 29th overall back in April, suffered his setback last week in a defeat to the Denver Broncos.

The New England Patriots have ruled out both wide receiver A.J. Brown (ankle/knee) and running back TreVeyon Henderson (illness) for Sunday's game against the Green Bay Packers.

They've listed tight end Hunter Henry — the team's receptions leader — as "questionable" with a foot issue.

Brown, whom the Patriots gave up a pair of picks for (including a 2028 first-rounder), has appeared in only three games this season. He suffered a high ankle sprain in the season opener and noted late last month that he wasn't 100 percent upon returning to game action.

As for Henderson, the second-year back revealed on Friday that he was hospitalized after experiencing "unexplained neurological symptoms."

Although the results of Henderson's examination were "positive and reassuring," doctors have yet to determine a cause, Henderson explained in his social media post.

Watson returned to practice in a limited capacity on Friday, but remains in concussion protocol. Monken said it's unclear if Watson will serve as the backup this week, though he will be listed as questionable

The Browns' No. 1 quarterback took a helmet-to-helmet hit during the first quarter of Sunday's loss to the Pittsburgh Steelers. Watson missed only four snaps, but later reported experiencing concussion symptoms on Monday night.

Los Angeles Rams edge rusher Myles Garrett is in line for a return to game action on the road Sunday against the Washington Commanders, according to NFL Network's Mike Garafolo and Ian Rapoport.

Garrett, coming back from early-season knee surgery that landed him on injured reserve, has practiced all week.

The two-time NFL Defensive Player of the Year, who's also the league's single-season sack record holder, went under the knife in the wake of the Rams' season-opening defeat to the San Francisco 49ers in Melbourne, Australia.

#2965. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2963. What we learned from the Spurs’ win over the Kings

Before the season and even into the first few games, critics have questioned the San Antonio Spurs' four-guard starting lineup. However, through 9 games, that lineup has a +25.4 net rating in 95 minutes and has led the Spurs to a 7-2 start. The bench lineups, especially those without Victor Wembanyama, have caused the team issues. That was never more apparent than in San Antonio's 124-109 thrashing of the Sacramento Kings on Friday night.

Let's be honest about the Kings for a moment. They are a bad basketball team, perhaps not in talent, but in discipline and execution. They lose people on defense and take errant shots on offense. Yet, multiple times on Friday, they gained back ground with most of San Antonio's starters off the floor.

Most of San Antonio's mistakes come from defense, where too often players, especially on the wings, overhelp on a drive or watch the ball, letting the offensive player get an open three or cut to the rim and score over the top of Luke Kornet. San Antonio's defensive lapses were a big reason the Kings shot 49% from the field and hit 46% from three. That second unit's defensive issues don't flip the game against the worst in the league, but against a legitimate, deep team, it could lead to a loss.

The Spurs will take a 15-point win on the road in their first NBA Cup game. But it's hard to come out of that game feeling overly confident about the performance. San Antonio has won seven games in a row, but over half of those have come against teams under .500. A real test awaits them on Saturday in the red-hot Portland Trail Blazers. The Spurs' second unit will need to step up its defensive play if they want to compete with another Western Conference leader.

Devin Vassell is one of the most important players on the Spurs. Vassell is shooting 34.1% from three this season, well below his usual average, which has hurt San Antonio's offense. When he's hitting shots, like he did against Sacramento, the offense opens up a ton for the guards and Wembanyama to dominate the paint. Vassell had 19 points, hitting 5 of his 6 threes on Friday. His off-ball defense was solid, too. He did about all you can ask from a glue guy in the starting lineup. San Antonio will need more performances like these moving forward.

The Kings ran one of the weakest zones I've seen this season, and it led to a big run from the Spurs in the first half. That said, it made me realize that San Antonio is basically built to bust zone defenses. One, Wembanyama is kind of the ultimate cheat code to beat a zone. He can stretch it out on the perimeter or punish smaller players who get caught helping around the basket in the zone. Then, the Spurs have dynamic playmaking guards who are big and physical and can operate in the middle of the zone, a common weak spot in many zone defenses. Add in a couple of knockdown shooters like Vassell and Champagnie, and it becomes nearly impossible to run zone against San Antonio. The Kings tried, and failed to do so on Friday.

Stephon Castle had a rough defensive night, but made up for it with some of the best playmaking we've seen from him this season. He had 19 points, 7 assists, and 1 turnover in the win. One of those assists came on a beautiful inverted pick-and-roll with Wembanyama as the ball handler and Castle as the screener. Castle caught the ball on the short roll, did a 180-degree turn, and fired the ball to a wide-open Vassell for three. It was as close to the Beautiful Game Spurs as San Antonio got on Friday.

Harrison Barnes has earned his spot in the rotation lately, and one could argue he should be getting more minutes than some other bench forward on the roster. He had 13 points on 4-for-7 shooting on Friday against his former team. Offensively, he's a savvy connective player who can take advantage of a defense that is out of position after one of the Spurs' guards creates an advantage. He looks more like the Barnes we saw in his first season with San Antonio, rather than last year. He has essentially replaced Carter Bryant in the rotation, and it doesn't look like he will be giving up that spot any time soon with how he is playing.

I'm interested to see how this team will look once Tobias Harris comes back (hopefully this month?). They desperately need another shot creator and capable defender in that second unit. I know a lot of people want to see him replace Vassell in the starting lineup, but it seems like he could provide even more value as one of the first subs in the game, then playing for a prolonged stretch alongside Kornet and one or two of the guards. The Spurs' top free-agent acquisition should give them more of the versatility many thought the team would have going into the season.

#2958. Luka Doncic's historic run jump-starts early MVP conversation

Ten games is too early for most valuable player conversation. Just don't tell fans at Crypto.com Arena.

"M-V-P! M-V-P!" the Lakers faithful chant at Luka Doncic every time the Lakers guard toes the free-throw line.

After dropping a season-high 53 points during the Lakers' 124-118 win over the Portland Trail Blazers on Friday, Doncic is averaging 40.1 points, 7.8 rebounds and 7.7 assists in a scoring binge for the ages. He's the fourth Laker to have four consecutive games of 40 or more points. He's the third to have seven games of 35 or more points.

And he's doing it efficiently: Doncic's sky-high shooting percentage dipped to 49.8% after an unusually cold 5-for-15 shooting night from three-point range Friday. He was still 12 for 18 from two-point range.

Here are three takeaways from Friday's win:

Lakers guard Luka Doncic shouts after sinking a three-pointer that gave the Lakers a five-point lead over Portland in the third quarter Friday at Crypto.com Arena. (Gina Ferazzi / Los Angeles Times)

Walker Kessler just shook his head as he grasped for the right words. There might not be many more left to describe what Doncic is doing.

"He's my MVP," the Lakers center said eventually.

Being in the most valuable player conversation isn't new for Doncic. The two-time scoring champion finished third in MVP voting in 2024 with Dallas. He averaged 33.9 points, 9.2 rebounds and 9.8 assists during the regular season, then led the Mavericks to the NBA Finals.

Each season the do-it-all guard is among the favorites to win the award. But Doncic's early-season campaign feels different. Not only is Doncic in great shape, Portland coach Micah Nori said before Friday's game, but Doncic also has the right pieces around him after the Lakers remade their roster to complement the star with floor-spacing shooters, strong perimeter defenders and a rolling center.

"It's a perfect offensive storm," Nori said. "He's the MVP right now. Hands down."

Lakers coach JJ Redick does not engage in MVP discussion until 50 games in, he said. When told Doncic topped NBA.com's first MVP ladder Friday, the Lakers coach brushed aside any chance to make a grand proclamation.

In the premature MVP rankings, Doncic was ahead of San Antonio Spurs center Victor Wembanyama. The 22-year-old is averaging 29.1 points, 12.6 rebounds, three assists and 4.2 blocks per game.

Doncic's 53-point outburst eclipsed Wembanyama's 52 points the star center scored Wednesday for the highest single-game scoring performance this season. But Wembanyama also grabbed 24 rebounds with eight blocks in a historic statline matched only by Wilt Chamberlain.

The French superstar finished second in MVP voting last year and was the first unanimous defensive player of the year in league history. His outsized defensive impact was a significant part of why he entered the season as the betting favorite to win his first MVP.

Read more: Swanson: Luka Doncic can lead Lakers to a title, but only if his teammates pull their weight

Doncic, who didn't get any votes from NBA general managers when asked in a preseason survey who they thought would win the MVP, said he's determined to step up on defense for his team. He is averaging 1.7 steals per game, which ranks second among Lakers players. His four drawn charges is tied for most in the league.

"He has a pride and he has a hunger," Lakers forward Sandro Mamukelashvili said of Doncic's defense. "He has a will to not be labeled as that guy who you can just attack."

Doncic is challenging Chamberlain's stats on the offensive end. His 361 points through his first nine games are the most for a player since Chamberlain's 472 points in 1962-63. No one has averaged 40 points over a season since the legendary center in 1963. The possibility has barely crossed Doncic's mind.

"As long as we win, I'm good," Doncic said.

The Lakers are 8-2 with the best record in the Western Conference. They have not started 8-2 since their last championship season.

Lakers forward Adou Thiero drives to the basket between Portland's Scoot Henderson (00) and Jrue Holiday (5) at Crypto.com Arena on Friday. (Gina Ferazzi / Los Angeles Times)

Adou Thiero had a loud four points, four rebounds and two assists in his long-awaited season debut Friday. The energetic forward missed the first nine games of the season because of swelling in his left knee. He didn't waste any time reintroducing himself to the team, keeping a loose ball alive on his first offensive possession that led to an offensive rebound and a put-back from Mamukelashvili.

"He literally did everything we asked him to do," Redick said.

The second-year forward has been hampered by his balky left knee since college, when he missed the end of his junior season after surgery. The procedure delayed the beginning of his rookie season and when he returned, a sprained MCL in his right knee knocked him out for six more weeks.

The injuries prevented Thiero from gaining any traction. This summer, when he had the longest stretch of sustained health during his short professional career, he could finally show teammates and coaches what he could do.

Thiero jumped out of the gym during practices. He smothered teammates while guarding the ball. He brought the same energy to the court Friday when it counted.

"We value Adou a lot," Kessler said. "He's a very physical player, really talented, athletic player."

Lakers forwards Ziaire Williams, left, and Sandro Mamukelashvili double team Portland guard Sidy Cissoko as he attempts a layup at Crypto.com Arena on Friday. (Gina Ferazzi / Los Angeles Times)

The Lakers missed their first 15 three-point shots. It felt like a minor miracle they trailed by only seven at halftime.

The most dynamic offense in the league had to rely on its defense. The Lakers forced 24 turnovers and held the Trail Blazers (6-2) to 15 points in the third quarter.

"It's nice to know when you're not making shots that you can also rely on your defense to help keep you in the game," said forward Jake LaRavia, who had seven points, eight rebounds and one steal.

The Lakers rank 21st in defensive rating, giving up 113.6 points per 100 possessions. They are one of the worst rebounding teams in the league, ranking 29th despite outrebounding the Trail Blazers by three. Their defense instead relies on blocks and steals.

The Lakers are tied for third in the NBA with 10.7 steals per game. Matisse Thybulle leads the team with 1.8 steals per game. Austin Reaves had a team-high four steals Friday.

#2957. OpenAI bugging suspect Alfozan pleads not guilty

The Saudi national charged with planting a listening device in the headquarters of OpenAI while he worked there pleaded not guilty on Friday during an appearance in San Francisco federal court.

Abdulrahman Alfozan, 35, was sent back to jail after the brief hearing. Alfozan has been held without bail since his arrest on a criminal complaint in early October.

He faces a maximum possible sentence of five years in prison if convicted in the case.

A grand jury indicted Alfozan on Oct. 27 on a single count of interception of oral communications, court records show.

Prosecutors say that he put a recording device in a television in an executive conference room at OpenAI's offices in San Francisco on Sept. 28.

Alfozan's lawyer declined to comment after the hearing before Magistrate Judge Peter Kang.

The judge ordered Alfozan to next appear in court on Nov. 17.

Alfozan is a venture partner in a Saudi Arabian venture capital firm, Raed Ventures.

"He currently serves as Engineering Lead at OpenAI, with a focus on AI Agents and the data platform," Alfozan's bio page on Raed's site says.

Prince Khalid Bin Bader Al Saud, a member of Saudi Arabia's royal family, is a venture partner in the same firm.

Alfozan's case comes as U.S. companies such as OpenAI are making rapid advancements in developing artificial intelligence agents, and as concerns grow about the potential theft of AI tech from those companies.In June, President Donald Trump, in an executive order on AI innovation, said it was U.S. policy "to protect American ingenuity and intellectual property from exploitation and theft by adversaries; and to cultivate America's advanced AI-enabled capabilities."Trump has had a close relationship with Saudi Arabia. But he has also taken steps during his second term in the White House to limit the number of visas for highly skilled foreign workers.OpenAI earlier this year held discussions with Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund, the country's sovereign wealth fund, about investing in the company.

— Jordan Novet reported from San Francisco

#2956. The race is on to build the first fusion power plant — and Europe is looking to Japan for help

The race is on to build the world's first commercial fusion power plant — and one of Europe's leading contenders in the field has just received a boost.

Munich-based Proxima Fusion signed memoranda of understanding with two Japanese industrial partners, Fujikura and Kyoto Fusioneering, in Tokyo on Oct. 28. Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi and German Chancellor Friedrich Merz — who was visiting in a bid to deepen trade ties — were both in attendance.

Ann Mettler, president of Catalyse Europe, a Brussels-based clean-energy think tank, and a former vice president of Breakthrough Energy, said developing the technology should be a strategic imperative for Europe.

"Europe is a resource-poor geography and we are very dependent on China. The world is in competition for technological supremacy — this is a zero-sum game," Mettler told CNBC, speaking in a personal capacity.

"Fusion is a silver bullet because the promise is abundant, clean, limitless energy."

Fusion energy seeks to replicate the way the sun produces power, using super-heated plasma to fuse two light atomic nuclei into one heavier one, releasing vast amounts of energy in the process.

There are two main approaches: one uses high-powered lasers, while the other uses powerful magnets to confine the plasma. Proxima is developing a type of magnetic device called a stellarator, which is designed to hold the plasma in place for long periods.

The agreement with Kyoto Fusioneering will expand existing work on next-generation gyrotrons — devices that generate high-power microwaves used to heat the plasma.

Proxima's agreement with 141-year-old Fujikura will explore plans for industrial-scale manufacturing in Germany of high-temperature superconducting, or HTS, tape, a specialized metallic tape that is superconducting at relatively high temperatures and can carry electrical current with virtually no resistive losses.

The tape is wound into coils to form powerful magnets that confine and control the heated plasma inside the stellarator.

Francesco Sciortino, CEO and co-founder of Proxima Fusion, told CNBC that Fujikura, which employs over 50,000 people worldwide, was a leader in manufacturing high-temperature superconductors, having built its reputation in optical fibers. "They think this tape has the potential to be the next optical fibre," he said.

He said Proxima, which is studying plans to build a manufacturing facility in Lower Saxony, hoped to make around 5,000 kilometers, or 3,100 miles, of tape annually by 2030.

The 140 million euro ($160 million) facility would be financed partly by the proceeds of a $468 million funding round in July, which valued Proxima, whose backers include Alphabet-owned Google, at $2.7 billion.

Proxima expects to need around 20,000 kilometers (12,400 miles) of the tape for its "Alpha" fusion demonstrator and a further 40,000 kilometers (24,900 miles) for its planned "Stellaris" fusion power station.

Sciortino said that establishing production of the tape in Germany was crucial as it would help Europe create the expertise and supply chains needed to turn its fusion research leadership into industrial capability.

This is where Europe has historically struggled, according to Catalyse Europe's Mettler.

"Every geography wants to turn leadership in science into industrial leadership and this is where Europe has traditionally been not so good — turning that scientific leadership into euros," she said.

While Europe had industrial giants to be proud of, disruptors tended to be more successful in frontier technologies, according to Mettler.

"If Europe is first to develop this technology, it will give us a huge advantage, but key to that will be building the supply chain," she said.

Sciortino said he remains confident Proxima will be the first company to turn on a pilot plant — but stressed the key to profitability would be making that plant as simple as possible to operate.

"You can have complexity in the design, manufacturing and operation of the plant," he said. "We can take care of the complexity in the design and manufacturing, but our view is that there needs to be simplicity in the operation. We are aiming for net energy gain during the early 2030s."

But Proxima has some well-heeled competitors. They include U.S.-based Commonwealth Fusion Systems, which is also backed by Google, and Helion Energy, as well as Canada's General Fusion. China has a number of state-backed players, most notably China Fusion Energy Co., while Tokyo-based Helical Fusion leads Japan's push.

"The supply chain in fusion energy needs to be built years before — people often think of frontier technologies as a switch that can be thrown," Mettler said. "That is not the case here."

#2954. Mississippi Senate: Longshot Ty Pinkins says he aimed to spoil Hyde-Smith, boost Colom vote

Longshot Mississippi Senate candidate Ty Pinkins said that at first wanted to win — but that he stayed in to spoil.

Pinkins told a local TV station that after an April poll showed he had no chance of winning he remained in the race in order to boost Democratic nominee Scott Colom's chances of beating Republican incumbent Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith.

Pinkins' purported strategy might pay off.

Colom is narrowly leading Hyde-Smith in ongoing counting by just 1,936 votes, a margin of just 0.2 percentage points, with 96.2% of the vote counted, according to the Associated Press. That margin is about one-tenth the size of Pinkins' share of the vote, 2.1 percentage points.

Pinkins has won just 20,010 votes so far out of the nearly 952,000 votes already counted.

"The only thing I did was stay in the race and let people shoot arrows at me," Pinkins said in an interview with WTVA in Mississippi on Thursday. "That is it. And that's a ... low price to pay to get someone in this race that I think can help people in the Delta and all across this state."

"The portion of the vote that I drew — and people were concerned about splitting the Black vote — what I actually did was split the Republican vote," he said.

Pinkins told the station in Tupelo that he intentionally avoided campaigning in areas with large Black populations to avoid cutting into the vote share of Colom, who, like Pinkins, is Black.

That tactic worked, he said, as the election results show he got most of his support from areas with relatively low Black populations.

"I'll be the boogeyman," he said. "But I'll tell you this: The independent in this race pulled most of his votes from a Republican, and for the first time since Reconstruction, a Democrat who is a Black man is leading."

Pinkins, a lawyer who previously ran for Mississippi secretary of state, was the Democratic nominee for Senate in 2024. He was crushed by Republican Sen. Roger Wicker, who won nearly 63% of the vote, compared with about 37% for Pinkins.

Despite that showing, the U.S. Army veteran decided to take another crack at the Senate in 2026.

But in the spring, he saw the writing on the wall for his candidacy.

"I got in this race to win," Pinkins told WTVA. "In April, I knew I was going to lose, but I also knew that I was pulling 70% of the Republican vote based on the [SPLC Action Fund and Impact Research] poll."

SPLC Action Fund, when it released the poll, said, "Sagging favorability numbers for Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith could open up a path to victory for Democrats."

"Independent Ty Pinkins has the potential to play spoiler for either candidate," SPLC Action Fund said in April.

In the WTVA interview, Pinkins said, "I probably attended maybe, maybe six events over the summer" as part of his campaign.

"Six events. I don't have to," Pinkins said. "Why? I'm not going to win. So why go to these Black communities that he's going to?"

Pinkins said all he had to do was visit counties that have relatively low Black populations and "talk to some Republicans that said they're going to vote for me."

"They were literally calling me and sending me emails: 'We're going to vote for you because we don't want to waste our vote on Cindy Hyde-Smith, we don't want to hold our nose and vote for her, so we're going to vote for you. Please stay in the race,'" he said.

The campaigns of Colom and Hyde-Smith did not immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

#2953. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2952. Notre Dame rises to CFP committee's challenge vs Miami. Did Fighting Irish lock in playoff?

When Notre Dame missed the College Football Playoff last season after losing a head-to-head matchup to Miami, the return of the rivalry in South Bend this year was circled by everyone within the Fighting Irish program.

The stakes only grew after Notre Dame fell in a top-10 showdown against BYU on Oct. 17, shrinking the Fighting Irish's margin for error in the 2026 College Football Playoff race and making their matchup against Miami a pivotal one for their postseason hopes.

Marcus Freeman's squad met the moment in Saturday's clash in South Bend, as No. 6 Notre Dame held on for a 31-24 win against top-ranked Miami behind an overall impressive game from CJ Carr.

Notre Dame vs Miami final score: Highlights from top-10 rivalry showdown

Carr, who took a slight shot for his performance against BYU from former Alabama coach Nick Saban on the "New Heights" podcast this week, delivered 319 passing yards and three touchdown passes in the win.

The win moves Notre Dame to 8-1 heading into the final three weeks of the regular season.

So, with a signature win in South Bend on Saturday, which will be celebrated for a while (or decades, given the history of the rivalry), has Freeman's squad done enough to lock up a spot in what could be the final 12-team CFP field before it doubles in size?

Given that the Irish entered Week 10 in a do-or-die situation for their CFP path, as the CFP committee placed them at No. 11 in Tuesday's initial rankings, it appears Notre Dame is a lock for the 12-team field.

Notre Dame lacked a signature on its CFP resume entering Saturday's matchup vs. Miami, as the Fighting Irish were unable to beat their lone ranked opponent earlier in the season in BYU — despite having a chance at the end. Freeman's squad now has that signature win against the No. 1 overall team in the country in all three major rankings: the US LBM Coaches Poll, the AP Top 25 and CFP top 25.

"They are a very good football team. But when you look at Notre Dame's schedule and the wins that they have vs. the teams that are ahead of them, they don't have that signature win right now," CFP committee chair Hunter Yurachek said on Tuesday on ESPN. "They lost in their only top 25 matchup at BYU. Their next best win is on a neutral site against Wisconsin.

"They've got a great opportunity in front of them this weekend at home versus Miami to get that signature win that I think they need to move up in the rankings. But the committee is very impressed by them. They wouldn't be 11th if we were not."

Saturday's win against Miami should be enough for the CFP committee to keep Notre Dame penciled into a spot in the top 11 of the CFP top 25 rankings until Selection Sunday on Dec. 6. That said, should Notre Dame drop one of its final three regular season games — vs. Boston College, SMU and Syracuse — things could get dicey again for the Fighting Irish as a two-loss team, just as it was a season ago.

Notre Dame also has something working in its favor for the 2026 CFP that it didn't have a year ago: The Fighting Irish have a memorandum of understanding with the College Football Playoff that guarantees them a berth in the 12-team field if they finish ranked No. 12 or better in the final CFP rankings.

#2951. Inside the card-grading empire shaping the price of Pokémon cards

Collectors may have more grading options than ever, but many still turn to Professional Sports Authenticator, or PSA — even as the industry leader works through a backlog of millions of cards.

Demand for grading — an act of verifying a card’s authenticity and condition — has grown as collectors increasingly treat trading and vintage sports cards as alternative investments.

PSA reported a backlog of 7.2 million items on Oct. 20, down from 12.4 million in July. The company suspended cheaper submission services in June to ease the backlog before reopening its Value service on Oct. 29.

Even so, some sellers would rather wait for a PSA label than send their cards to a competitor, dealers told CNBC.

“It really all comes down to secondary market values,” said Geoff Wilson, founder and CEO of CardsHQ, which operates one of the world's largest sports card shops.

PSA-graded cards typically command higher prices than equivalent grades from competitors, Wilson said, giving collectors and investors an incentive to choose PSA in hopes of a stronger return.

The financial difference can be substantial.

A 1999 Base Set Charizard graded PSA 9 sold for about $3,440 on eBay on Sept. 29, compared with roughly $1,890 for a CGC 9 copy sold the same day, according to CNBC's review of PriceCharting records.

That pricing gap reinforces PSA's lead and gives its decisions about fees and submission services considerable influence over the resale market.

PSA had not responded to CNBC’s detailed questions at the time of publication. In published updates, the company has attributed service restrictions and pricing changes to demand exceeding capacity, and outlined investments intended to reduce the backlog.

Grading companies verify a card’s authenticity, assess its condition and seal it in a protective plastic case, known as a slab. PSA uses a 10-point scale, with its highest grade, Gem Mint 10, reserved for cards it describes as "virtually perfect."

Like a credit rating in bond markets, a card grade gives buyers and sellers a common benchmark for assessing condition.

That can reduce friction in transactions by giving both sides a reference point based on past sales of the same card at the same grade.

That capability has helped open the trading-card market to more global participation and made cross-border transactions easier, according to an unnamed spokesperson for Singapore-based submission dealer Oxley Grading.

The spokesperson attributed PSA’s position as the industry’s “gold standard” to its early start, brand recognition and extensive history of completed sales.

“They've just built up such a strong branding and a lot of sales records,” the spokesperson said.

They estimated that roughly 90% of the graded cards offered to Oxley Grading for purchase carry PSA’s label.

The dominance creates a self-reinforcing cycle: buyers seek out PSA-graded cards, giving sellers an incentive to use the company. The abundance of PSA slabs in shops and online marketplaces further reinforces the brand's visibility and demand.

For a seller choosing a grader, that established resale demand can outweigh a competitor’s lower fees or faster turnaround times.

PSA sits within a larger collectibles business.

Its parent company, Collectors, also owns rival grading brands Beckett and SGC, pricing-data platform Card Ladder, the PSA Vault and financial services. The vault links storage with selling through external marketplaces, including eBay.

Together, those businesses span much of a card’s commercial journey — grading, pricing, storage, sales and even financing against collectibles.

Collectors' three card-grading brands accounted for roughly 71% of August grading volume among the major firms tracked by industry analyst GemRate. PSA alone represented about 65%, while the next-largest player, CGC, represented about 28%.

Collectors' consolidation has also drawn legal scrutiny.

A proposed class action filed in April challenges Collectors’ acquisitions of SGC and Beckett, alleging that the deals unlawfully reduced competition in card grading. The allegations have not been proven in court.

Collectors has defended the Beckett acquisition as a way to strengthen a trusted brand while preserving collectors’ ability to choose the grading service that best suits their items.

“We believe that strong, well-supported brands help grow access to the hobby,” Collectors CEO Nat Turner said in the company's 2025 acquisition announcement.

For sellers who prioritize PSA's resale premium, changes to its fees and submission services can alter the economics of grading a card.

PSA raised fees across several existing tiers in February, including Value Bulk from $21.99 to $24.99 and Value from $27.99 to $32.99. It also lengthened estimated turnaround times for some services, saying the changes would help balance submission demand with grading capacity.

The suspension of cheaper tiers in June further narrowed affordable options, while higher costs have made customers more selective about which cards they submit, according to Oxley Grading's spokesperson.

Meanwhile, Wilson said the restrictions have particularly affected collectors and dealers who buy cards ungraded, send them to PSA and then resell them at a higher price.

For cards worth a few hundred dollars or less, that strategy has become harder to justify, he said.

“You're seeing more raw cards bought and sold at card shows,” Wilson added, using the industry term for ungraded cards.

PSA has acknowledged that customers are waiting too long.

In an Oct. 28 update, the company said submissions in April and May were 2.5 times the volume received in the same months a year earlier. It also announced shorter turnaround estimates for Standard and Priority services, while saying new Value submissions would be processed behind existing orders.

“We have added an incredible amount of capacity, but we still have work to do,” Ryan Hoge, president of PSA and grading at Collectors, said in the statement.

For Wilson, those investments in capacity are part of PSA's appeal.

“They've made the collector experience better by doing that,” he said.

The leading graders' dominance may leave collectors with little room to push back.

Collectors' preference for PSA's resale value can make switching graders less attractive, the Oxley Grading's spokesperson said, adding, "PSA's lead comes from resale demand and liquidity, and other labels are still building that."

Overseas customers can face fewer practical grading options as many major graders are based in the U.S., Amber Chuah, operations manager of trading-card platform TCG Marketplace, told CNBC.

The complaints she hears most often involve long waits and disappointment when cards receive lower grades than customers expected.

Challenging a grade can require another round of shipping and waiting, discouraging some collectors from disputing the result, she added.

"If you're really keen on selling, that's a lot of time."

PSA did not respond to CNBC's questions about the sources' claims.

PSA’s terms allow customers to request a paid review of disputed grades, with customers responsible for grading and applicable shipping fees. The company also offers defect notes for eligible submissions to provide more detail on how a grade was determined.

CardsHQ's Wilson said some variation between assessments is an inherent challenge in a process that involves human judgment.

Wilson pushes back against the suggestion that PSA’s position leaves customers without alternatives.

“First of all, there are alternatives,” he said, pointing to CGC, Beckett and smaller grading firms such as TAG.

CGC’s grading volume in August was more than double its level a year earlier, according to GemRate, showing that rival graders are attracting business. Beckett also recorded strong growth, although it is owned by Collectors.

Oxley Grading's spokesperson said there may be room for lower-cost services aimed at collectors who care more about a card’s artwork, sentimental value or preservation than maximizing its resale value.

Competing for sellers focused on the strongest financial return remains more difficult.

Building comparable resale acceptance would require substantial investment, with no guarantee that buyers would ultimately value a newcomer's label as highly as PSA's, the spokesperson said.

For Wilson, broader acceptance in the resale market is key to shifting that balance.

“If the market as a whole starts to respect the value of the other grading companies' cards more, then collectors will increasingly submit to those companies,” he said.
