#2894. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#2893. America doesn't like socialism. So why are socialists winning?

Democratic socialists just had their biggest election night yet.

There's just one complication: Most American voters don't actually like socialism.

Six candidates backed by the Democratic Socialists of America won House seats Tuesday, giving the movement its largest foothold yet in Congress and greater clout in a Democratic Party already divided over how far left to go.

The victories, coming on the heels of New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani's rise to power, mark another milestone for a movement that has spent years challenging the party establishment. Now, with Democrats sweeping control of Congress, the question is whether those electoral gains will translate into actual political power.

The results of the new CNBC Voter Poll show the potential hazard of handing too much power to the party's left flank. The survey of roughly 80,000 midterm voters found that 53% view socialism unfavorably, compared with 33% who viewed it favorably. Capitalism fared considerably better, with 56% expressing a positive view. Just 18% of voters identified themselves as democratic socialists.

That disconnect underscores both the opportunity and the challenge facing Democrats and their growing left flank. How the party reconciles the divide between reforming capitalism and fundamentally overhauling it could shape its legislative agenda in Congress, its economic message to voters and, ultimately, its direction heading into the 2028 presidential election.

The DSA's presence in Congress will be quadrupled when Tuesday's victors, all of whom won in safely Democratic districts, are sworn in.

But the projected Democratic landslide of more than 250 House seats could dilute their influence. Party leaders could afford to lose all eight DSA votes on most measures, leaving the group with far less bargaining power than it would wield in a narrowly controlled chamber.

Still, the movement is building financial muscle. While the DSA relies on membership dues, wealthy donors are now backing its candidates through outside groups. The 12 largest donors to American Priorities, a super PAC supporting some DSA-backed candidates, gave nearly $5.9 million this cycle, according to a CNBC analysis of Federal Election Commission filings through Oct. 31.

"We've had success with more donors but also we have an even stronger ground game to spread our message," said Megan Romer, co-chair of the DSA. "Mailers and ads alone just don't do it anymore. You have to knock on more doors and make more phone calls and talk to more people at work and in your union."

The more revealing tests on Tuesday came outside of liberal enclaves.

In Florida, DSA member Angie Nixon came within three percentage points of an upset in a state where Democrats haven't won a Senate race since 2012 and President Donald Trump won by 13 percentage points in 2024. But she lost by a wider margin than Democratic gubernatorial candidate David Jolly, who took a more moderate approach.

Michigan told a similar story. Progressive Abdul El-Sayed – backed by self-described democratic socialist Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., – won his Senate seat by seven percentage points. Yet he lagged nearly four percentage points behind Democratic Gov.-elect Jocelyn Benson's winning margin.

El-Sayed shares many DSA priorities but neither belongs to the group nor received its endorsement. He says he favors reforming capitalism over replacing it.

State ballot initiatives offered an equally mixed ideological verdict: Nearly two-thirds of voters in ruby-red Montana backed limits on corporate political spending, while deep-blue California rejected a billionaire wealth tax by roughly 10 percentage points, even as voters approved higher income taxes on the wealthy.

The results sharpen the dilemma over Democrats' identity heading into 2028, as Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, D-N.Y., the DSA's most prominent standard-bearer, seemingly weighs a White House bid: Can left-wing populism expand the party's coalition without alienating the swing-state moderates it needs to win a presidential election – especially in a less favorable political environment?

"I think a lot of people did vote for Democrats who would not ordinarily and who probably did it ... with their teeth gritted," Romer told CNBC.

But it's clear that even voters who reject socialism aren't satisfied with capitalism as it stands.

Three out of every four participants in the CNBC Voter Poll said the condition of the U.S. economy was poor or not so good, compared with just 2% who described it as excellent. And 70% said the economic system favors the wealthy rather than treating most Americans fairly.

That frustration helped fuel a populist message far beyond the socialist left, with victorious Democrats from Adam Hamilton in Kansas to James Talarico in Texas tapping into anger over rising costs, economic inequality and the political influence of wealthy elites. Eighteen Sanders-endorsed candidates also won, including in traditionally right-leaning states like Ohio and Montana.

"The real fight in this country is not left versus right. It is top versus bottom," Talarico, who flipped a Senate seat for Democrats in the Lone Star state, said in his victory speech. "The people at the top work so hard to keep us angry and divided because our unity is a threat to their wealth and their power."

Even politicians who weren't on the ballot took note. Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., said in a video posted to X Friday that "Democrats won by being populist economic fighters."

Economists have pointed to measures showing increasing wealth concentration and a shrinking share of the U.S. GDP heading to workers in the form of pay as signs that the divide between the country's rich and poor is widening. Coming out of the pandemic, the "K"-shaped economy has become a common descriptor for the unequal recovery seen by income classes.

Americans displayed their economic dissatisfaction well before they headed to the polls this week. The University of Michigan's closely followed consumer sentiment index hit all-time lows this year.

"We have a rising tide that is not equally lifting all boats," said Joe Brusuelas, chief economist at RSM, a tax firm. "That's not what we used to tell ourselves when we were young."

Rather than signaling a distaste for capitalism as a whole, Brusuelas said this week's results show voters want a system that also includes populist policies like Medicare for all. Or, as he calls it, "capitalism with a human face."

"These things are not mutually exclusive," said Brusuelas, who regularly briefs members of Congress on policy issues. "We can walk and chew gum at the same time."

Tuesday's Democratic sweep left some on Wall Street racing to prepare their clients' portfolios for potential policy shifts. Bank of America analysts detailed what heightened scrutiny of social media platforms or artificial intelligence data centers could mean for the shares of companies in those industries. CIBC built a basket of stocks that should benefit from a Democratically controlled Congress.

Still, the reaction of Wall Street veterans and New York City business moguls to this week's election was relatively subdued compared with Mamdani's victory in the mayoral race last year.

After Mamdani clinched his party's nomination, Pershing Square CEO Bill Ackman said he was "a bit depressed." Ahead of Mamdani's victory, Kathryn Wylde, who leads the Partnership for New York City, a nonprofit group of business executives, told CNBC that "terror is the feeling" as he rose in the polls.

Wall Street magnates wondered how a city known as the epicenter of financial markets and global capitalism could survive under a self-described socialist. Some questioned if billionaires would flee the city or whether corporations would move their headquarters — fears that largely haven't materialized since Mamdani took office.

With roughly 10 months of Mamdani's term in the rearview mirror, Anthony Chan, the former chief economist at JPMorgan, said finance moguls have realized that politics don't necessarily result in material policy changes. Chan said it's a message they've carried with them exiting this week, with many understanding that any incoming lawmakers in Washington aligned with democratic socialist policies would be a small minority.

"Many of the people that I meet that were very adamantly against and opposed to his candidacy now are sending their kids to his free daycare centers," Chan said, referencing Mamdani's partnership with the state to expand no-cost programs for 2-year-olds in New York City.

"I thought you felt that the world was going to end if this mayor came in," said Chan, who's also an alumnus of Barclays and the New York Federal Reserve. "Now, you're sending your kids to his free daycare centers."

But the DSA has even bigger ambitions for 2028. With growing political clout, infrastructure and financial backing, the movement is looking to expand its influence nationwide, while one of its most prominent allies, Ocasio-Cortez, weighs a White House run.

"That infrastructure is there, and that will make a big difference for a potential left presidential candidate," Romer said. "It's really kind of do or die time. We really have to deliver."

#2892. Sixers-Cavs by the numbers: Pressure, threes and a 29-point rematch

Sixers-Cavs by the numbers: Pressure, threes and a 29-point rematch originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers and Cavaliers have already played one game that looked like two.

Cleveland led by 29 points during the Sixers' home opener on Oct. 22 and carried a 69-45 advantage into halftime. Philadelphia responded by winning the second half, 69-37, and completing a 114-106 comeback.

It was the Sixers' largest comeback since the NBA began maintaining detailed play-by-play data in 1997-98. Only nine other teams during that span had overcome a deficit of at least 29 points.

Fifteen days later, the teams will meet again Friday night at Rocket Arena. This time, the matchup counts toward the NBA Cup standings.

The 8-1 Sixers have won five consecutive games and opened group play with a 98-93 victory over the Knicks. The 4-4 Cavs have dropped two straight and will make their Cup debut.

Here are five numbers that help frame the rematch.

Philadelphia's comeback required more than a hot shooting stretch.

The Sixers committed 15 turnovers in the first half, allowing Cleveland to build a 22-6 advantage in points off turnovers. Their pressure then changed the game after halftime.

The Cavs committed 21 turnovers by the end of the night. What had been a 16-point Cleveland advantage in points off turnovers finished even at 22 apiece.

"They upped their pressure and physicality," Cavs head coach Kenny Atkinson said after the game. "It was pretty clear. We did not handle it well."

The Sixers scored 64 of their 69 second-half points through their starting lineup. Tyrese Maxey finished with 28 points, and Philadelphia turned a game that appeared finished into a franchise-record comeback.

The first half of the opening meeting now looks like an exception.

The Sixers have committed 12.9 turnovers per 100 possessions this season, the NBA's second-lowest rate. They are also third in turnover differential, averaging 3.4 fewer giveaways than their opponents.

That is notable for a team that entered the season intending to play faster. Philadelphia has pushed the ball without routinely surrendering possessions.

LeBron James' presence as the primary facilitator has helped. So has Maxey's willingness to move the ball when defenses trap him. Joel Embiid has averaged 5.2 assists, third among qualified centers, and the Sixers have increasingly trusted VJ Edgecombe to make decisions late in games.

Cleveland's defense has not forced many mistakes. The Cavs enter Friday near the bottom of the league in opponent turnover rate.

The first meeting swung when the Sixers stopped giving away possessions and forced Cleveland into mistakes of its own.

The Sixers have allowed 105.1 points per 100 possessions, the NBA's second-best defensive rating. Cleveland enters with the league's ninth-ranked offense.

The Cavs are the only top-10 offense the Sixers have faced through their first nine games.

Philadelphia has held opponents to a 50 percent effective field-goal percentage and 31.8 percent shooting from three-point range. Its defensive shot profile has been encouraging, too. Only 46 percent of opposing three-point attempts have been classified as wide open, the fifth-lowest share in the league, and opponents have taken just 22 percent of their threes from the corners.

There is reason for caution. Based on the quality of shots Philadelphia has allowed, only Washington's opponents have underperformed their expected effective field-goal percentage by a wider margin. The Sixers have defended well, but opponents are unlikely to remain this cold indefinitely.

Cleveland, with Donovan Mitchell, James Harden and Evan Mobley, can test whether those numbers hold against high-level individual shot creation.

The Cavs have attempted exactly 42 three-pointers per game and made 37.2 percent of them.

Cleveland went 7 for 29 from beyond the arc in Philadelphia. Mitchell, Harden and De'Andre Hunter combined to shoot 3 for 17. The Cavs scored 60 points in the paint and still reached 106, but their 24.1 percent performance from three prevented them from stopping the Sixers' comeback.

Friday's game could look considerably different if Cleveland generates its normal volume.

The Sixers have controlled the three-point line well so far, but Mitchell is coming off a 27-point performance against Oklahoma City and scored 32 in the first meeting with Philadelphia. Harden added 16 points that night, although his six turnovers became costly as the Sixers increased their pressure.

Philadelphia has been outrebounded by 5.1 per game. The Cavs rank sixth with 47.5 rebounds per contest.

The Sixers have already shown they can survive a major deficit on the glass. They beat Milwaukee by 11 despite getting outrebounded by 22. That is not a formula they will want to test regularly against Cleveland's size.

Mobley had 18 points and eight rebounds in the first meeting. Jarrett Allen added 14 points and seven boards, helping Cleveland win the rebounding battle, 46-44.

Whether Allen plays Friday remains uncertain.

Cleveland listed Allen as questionable because of a right knee injury after he left Wednesday's loss to Oklahoma City in the first quarter. Allen collided knee-to-knee with Thunder forward Chet Holmgren and did not return.

The Cavs also listed Peyton Watson as questionable with right hamstring soreness and Tyrese Proctor as questionable with an ankle injury. Proctor left Wednesday's game during the fourth quarter. Jaylon Tyson is out with a right ankle sprain.

Allen's availability could significantly alter the matchup on the glass. If he sits, Thomas Bryant would likely handle more of the center minutes behind Mobley. If he plays, Cleveland will have the same starting frontcourt that outrebounded the Sixers during the first meeting.

Atkinson has emphasized the possession battle during Cleveland's two-game slide.

"You've got to get more shots than the other team," he said this week. "Rebounding is a big part of it."

That also carries added importance in the NBA Cup, where point differential is the second tiebreaker after head-to-head results.

Philadelphia enters Friday at 1-0 with a plus-five differential in East Group B. Miami is also 1-0 at plus-20, while Cleveland is playing its first group game.

The Sixers already know they can erase 29 points against the Cavs. A cleaner start would be far more useful than proving it twice.

#2891. How Patriots legend Rob Gronkowski truly felt about Tom Brady’s presence at his New England Hall of Fame ceremony

How Patriots legend Rob Gronkowski truly felt about Tom Brady's presence at his New England Hall of Fame ceremony appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

Rob Gronkowski had plenty of familiar faces in his corner for his New England Patriots Hall of Fame induction on Saturday, but one presence clearly stood out above the rest. The former tight end opened up about what it meant to have Tom Brady by his side for the big day.

"It meant everything… He wanted to be here. It just meant a lot, man. Tom can be anywhere else. He's the greatest player of all time. It just meant a lot to myself, my family, and to the fans, I'm sure as well," Gronkowski said, via Justin Turpin.

Brady received a loud ovation from the crowd at Patriot Place Plaza, where many of Gronkowski's former teammates and coaches gathered for the ceremony. The legendary quarterback also showed his former tight end some love on social media, reposting a video of the two embracing with a simple message.

The mutual admiration makes sense. Brady and Gronkowski formed one of the most dominant quarterback-tight end duos in NFL history, winning three Super Bowls together in New England before reuniting with the Tampa Bay Buccaneers to capture a fourth title in the 2020 season.

Gronkowski was voted into the Patriots Hall of Fame by fans in April, in his first year of eligibility. He joined Brady, who was inducted in 2024.

The honor caps a remarkable career in New England. Gronkowski spent nine seasons with the Patriots after being selected in the second round of the 2010 NFL Draft out of Arizona. He racked up 521 receptions for 7,861 yards and 79 touchdowns in the regular season, and his 80 total touchdowns rank first in franchise history. He was also a five-time Pro Bowler and four-time First-Team All-Pro.

Patriots owner Robert Kraft was unable to attend the ceremony while recovering from back surgery, but he had high praise for Gronkowski when the honor was announced.

"Rob became a fan favorite almost immediately and remained the standard at tight end for nearly a decade," Kraft said.

The celebration continues on Sunday, when Gronkowski will be honored at halftime of the Patriots' game against the Green Bay Packers. Brady will be on hand for that one as well, calling the game from the FOX broadcast booth.

#2887. Berkshire Hathaway more than doubles buybacks in third quarter as earnings decline

Berkshire Hathaway said Saturday it accelerated its share repurchases during the third quarter, as operating and overall profits took a hit.

The Greg Abel-led conglomerate bought back $9.6 billion worth of Berkshire stock. That's more than double the $4.5 billion repurchased in the second quarter and brings the company's total buybacks for 2026 to about $14.4 billion.

The sharp increase came as Berkshire's operating earnings — those from wholly-owned businesses — fell year over year, as profits from its insurance business pulled back.

Berkshire operating profits totaled $12.705 billion, down 5.8% from $13.485 billion in the year-earlier period. Insurance underwriting tumbled 43% year on year to $1.352 billion, while insurance investment income declined 14% to $2.734 billion. Berkshire's railroad, energy and manufacturing businesses all saw earnings grow year over year.

The company's overall earnings, which include Berkshire's sprawling stock market portfolio, also dropped 28% in the third quarter to $22.112 billion from $30.796 billion a year prior. Investment gains for the company fell nearly 50% to $9.407 billion.

Despite the earnings pullback, Berkshire stock has outperformed recently amid a massive rotation out of technology stocks. Class A shares are up more than 12% over the past month, while the S&P 500 has dropped 5.5% in that time. Year to date, Berkshire has gained 13%, while the broad market index is up just 7.8%.

Berkshire's five largest equity holdings remained the same in the third quarter: Alphabet, American Express, Apple, Bank of America and Coca-Cola.

Berkshire's cash hoard also grew 3% in the third quarter $377.435 billion from $365.514 billion in the second quarter. However, excluding rail and utility cash along with T-bill payables, the conglomerate's cash holdings totaled $352.3 billion, down 1.9% quarter over quarter.

— CNBC's Alex Crippen contributed reporting.

#2885. Risk of Iran war escalation remains high despite Democratic sweep in U.S. midterm elections

The U.S. and Iran are careening toward renewed war despite growing hopes this week that the Democrats' decisive midterm election victory will bring the conflict to an end, analysts say.

Crude oil prices fell more than 16% over the last five trading days after U.S. Vice President JD Vance publicly blamed the Republicans' stinging loss on the war. Vance's remarks raised questions about whether the administration still has support to continue the conflict with Iran.

The Democrats plan to use their comfortable majorities in both chambers of Congress to pass a war powers resolution that demands President Donald Trump end the war. But tensions in the Middle East have not eased since the election, and the Democrats aren't sworn in until January, the analysts say.

"Throughout the week, we were struck by the divergence between deepening escalation concerns in the Gulf and the headline-driven off-ramp optimism of market participants thousands of miles away," Helima Croft, head of global commodity strategy at RBC Capital Markets, told investors in a note Friday.

Late Saturday, U.S. Central Command said it disabled a St. Kitts-flagged ship after the tanker ignored multiple warnings and attempted to leave the region after visiting an Iranian port, according to media reports. Iran state media also reported on explosions on Qeshm Island in the Strait of Hormuz, as well as several blasts occurring in the port of Jask. CNBC couldn't independently confirm the reports.

Several oil tankers have also come under attack near Oman since the election, killing one seafarer, according to maritime security agencies. Iran's Houthi allies in Yemen continue to fire missiles at neighboring Saudi Arabia.

Meanwhile, the U.S. military buildup continues, with a third carrier strike group en route to the region. The consulting group Rapidan Energy sees an 80% chance that fighting will resume as diplomacy stalls.

Trump told reporters Thursday that he will make a decision soon on whether to attack Iran again.

"I would rather not destroy the rest of the country if possible but maybe I'll have to," Trump said in the Oval Office. "You'll know soon enough."

"We're locked and loaded," the president said. "If we have to do the rest of the Iran thing, we're very much locked in."

Crude oil prices will vault above $100 a barrel again in response to renewed fighting, said Bob McNally, president of Rapidan and a former energy advisor to President George W. Bush. Brent crude, the international benchmark, traded above $97 on Friday, while U.S. crude was around $90.

"President Donald Trump is determined as a legacy item, almost at all costs, to prevent Iran from getting a nuclear weapon, having them give up their nuclear capabilities," McNally told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" on Friday.

"The Iranian regime, on the other hand, is not willing to do it," the analyst said. The escalation dynamic remains in place so long as that core difference between the U.S. and Iran persists, he said.

The risk of war is still alive, especially since Trump no longer faces future elections, said Amos Hochstein, a former energy advisor to President Joe Biden. Gasoline, diesel and jet fuel prices would all surge as a consequence, Hochstein told CNBC's "Power Lunch" Friday.

Tehran has been taunting the Trump administration since the election. The social media account of Iran's Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei said the "process of the U.S.'s collapse has accelerated" after the midterm results.

And Congress is unlikely to pass a war powers resolution until Democrats take control in January. Trump can veto such a resolution anyway.

The main leverage Congress will have is the power of the purse, Croft said. Government funding expires on Dec. 11, and negotiations for another continuing resolution could drag into 2027, she said. This would give Democrats more leverage when they take power in January, the analyst said.

"Importantly, deferring funding decisions would not immediately constrain Trump's ability to conduct military operations, but could significantly reshape the administration's longer-term funding outlook," Croft told investors.

Trump discussed diplomatic efforts to de-escalate tensions in the region with the emir of Qatar during a Friday phone call, according to Qatar's state news agency.

An unnamed senior Iranian official told Reuters on Thursday that Tehran sent a nuclear proposal to the U.S. through Qatar. Tehran will only discuss the proposal after the U.S. meets its demands, the Iranian official told Reuters.

Trump seemed to dismiss the diplomatic efforts Thursday.

"Iran will never have a nuclear weapon," the president said. "They're not doing anything without our approval. It doesn't make any difference what they're looking at," he said of the diplomatic efforts.

#2882. Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."

#2881. Observations after Sixers earn strong road win over Pistons, Brown scores 30

Observations after Sixers earn strong road win over Pistons, Brown scores 30 originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers remain the Eastern Conference's sole leader.

They earned a 112-106 road win over the Pistons on Wednesday night to move to 8-1 on the season and stay at the top of the standings. Detroit fell to 6-2.

The Sixers last started 8-1 in the 2023-24 season, which was Nick Nurse's first as the team's head coach.

Jaylen Brown scored a season-high 30 points and pulled down seven rebounds.

Joel Embiid had 26 points and a season-best 15 rebounds. LeBron James posted 13 points, 10 rebounds and eight assists. Tyrese Maxey added 19 points and seven assists.

The Pistons' leading scorers were Duncan Robinson with 27 points and Cade Cunningham with 26.

The Sixers were down Dominick Barlow (lower back stress reaction) and Ariel Hukporti (right Achilles tendon tear). The Pistons' injured players included Jalen Duren (right hamstring strain) and Isaiah Joe (right hip contusion).

Nurse told reporters in Detroit he thinks "it'll be another 10 to 12 days before they kind of clear (Barlow) for getting on the court to see where he is," according to Sixers Wire's Ky Carlin.

The Sixers will visit the Cavaliers on Friday night. Here are observations on their victory over Detroit:

Embiid assisted the Sixers' first basket with a sharp pass out of a double team. He saw Brown's cut along the baseline and dished to him for a layup.

Detroit ran several double drag screen actions early, forcing Embiid to defend on the perimeter. The Pistons started 7 for 11 from the floor and took a 17-11 edge on a Robinson three-pointer.

The Sixers then made a 10-0 run and Embiid was right in the middle of it. He converted a put-back layup after a pretty high-low feed from James. Embiid hit two foul shots to give the Sixers the lead. The team raised its defensive level and Detroit's offense grew frigid.

Embiid was very good on the glass in the first half, grabbing eight rebounds. The Sixers had an excellent rebounding game as a team, limiting the Pistons to four offensive boards over 48 minutes.

Embiid didn't break out of his early-season three-point shooting slump, going 0 for 3. He's 3 for 26 (11.5 percent) on the season and hasn't made a three since the Sixers' second game.

Late in the second quarter, Embiid got into a dead-ball dust-up with Tolu Smith. After an officials' review, Embiid was assessed a technical foul for "initiating" the altercation.

Playing on his 26th birthday, Maxey missed his first four field goals and was scoreless until he hit a floater with 31.5 seconds left in the first quarter.

Brown picked up the scoring slack, tallying 10 points in the first on 4-for-6 shooting and hitting a deep, buzzer-beating three to put the Sixers up 29-21. He produced at all three levels, chose smart spots to attack and looked comfortable in the rhythm of the Sixers' offense.

Meanwhile, James did his usuall stellar work as a passer. He assisted two Adem Bona slams in the second quarter and constantly spotted open teammates. Nurse rewarded Bona's strong opening stint, playing him for a lengthy stretch.

Brown swished a corner triple to extend the Sixers' lead to 48-31. With a 3-for-5 night, Brown is at 45.7 percent from long distance so far this season. It wouldn't be surprising at all if Brown's three-point shooting improved from last season's 34.7 percent clip. With such an abundance of star power, the Sixers' hope will be that everyone gets regular open looks beyond the arc throughout the year.

As for Maxey, he sat with 3:26 left in the second quarter after being whistled for his third foul. Even with a mere two points from Maxey, the Sixers entered halftime holding a 14-point advantage.

The Pistons played better basketball to begin the third quarter.

Following a John Collins alley-oop jam, Nurse called timeout with his team's lead down to single digits. The Pistons' offense sustained some success, but the Sixers prevented a sustained run.

Embiid played an especially physical, aggressive third quarter. He tipped in his own miss and Maxey then sunk a three to make the Sixers' lead 84-68. They were up as many as 17 points in the third.

Embiid sat out the fourth quarter Monday night for the Sixers' win over the Bulls and said he was on a minutes restriction (25) because of his right foot plantar fasciitis. He played the fourth period against the Pistons and logged 33 minutes. Bona went back to the locker room early in the fourth. Nurse told reporters postgame that Bona had "neck soreness."

Detroit did not let the Sixers coast to victory.

Robinson drained a three. Cunningham made a pair of free throws and drove in for a layup that cut the Pistons' deficit to 106-102.

The Sixers halted the Pistons' run before anything disastrous was on the table. Brown blocked a Cunningham three-point try, Embiid knocked down two foul shots, and the East's No. 1 early-season team improved to 4-0 on the road.

#2880. Anthropic IPO meets AI backlash after midterms show stinging rebuke of data centers

Americans were unequivocal at the ballot box this week as they expressed disdain for tech billionaires and mistrust of artificial intelligence. With that backdrop, Anthropic is about to try to go public.

The AI company is gearing up for a potentially historic IPO, likely targeting mid-November for its Nasdaq debut. CNBC reported on Friday that Anthropic investors see the company aiming for a valuation of $1 trillion to $1.5 trillion, down from a target of $2 trillion that had been floated in recent months.

From Wall Street to Main Street, it's becoming a tougher environment for Anthropic. The Nasdaq on Thursday suffered its steepest drop in 19 months, and it's down to start November after October marked its worst month since March 2025. Chipmakers and neoclouds, the companies most closely tied to the AI buildout, are getting hit the hardest.

Voters rebuked AI in Tuesday's midterm elections, while sending Democrats to sweeping congressional victories across the country. Candidates who advocated for more stringent AI regulation won handily in states like Michigan, Ohio, Texas and Georgia, which will all now have Democratic governors, and local communities opposed data center-related ballot measures in 40 out of 41 cases.

More than half of voters said AI data centers were a "crucial" or "major factor" in who they supported for Congress, according to an exit poll from NBC News.

"People don't want a data center going near them if it's just going to house AI that's going to help a big tech company profit," Maura Austin, a behavioral scientist at the University of Virginia, told CNBC. "What Anthropic is doing when they're going public with such a high valuation is they're really leaning into that big tech, billionaire kind of caricature. I expect that's not going to go well with the public."

Anthropic has long tried to position itself as a safety-conscious company that takes AI's potential risks and benefits seriously. CEO Dario Amodei co-founded the company alongside a handful of researchers who defected from OpenAI, now its chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, and it primarily sells its products and services to other businesses.

The company's enterprise focus has helped it avoid becoming the public face of AI, leaving that distinction to OpenAI and CEO Sam Altman. But with Anthropic poised to become the first pure-play AI company to hit the public market, minting a number of new billionaires and many more millionaires in the process, a heightened level of scrutiny is coming.

"Being a public company, just that by itself pushes you in the limelight," Reena Aggarwal, a professor of finance at Georgetown University, said in an interview. "And then AI is such a hot-button issue that Anthropic will be in the limelight even more."

Matt Kennedy, a senior IPO market strategist at Renaissance Capital, said that's not necessarily a bad thing for Anthropic.

Public markets force greater transparency, he said, and the company could attract new users through its debut. And since AI has become such a mainstream conversation topic, several private companies are already household names.

"I would say that a public company does not deserve more criticisms than a comparable private company," Kennedy said. "So Anthropic should not be put in a harsher public spotlight than OpenAI just because it's public."

AI backlash accelerated over the summer after a series of high-profile safety and security incidents rattled the public. As concerns mounted, the debate over data centers intensified, while tech's hyperscalers simultaneously boosted their forecasts for capital expenditures, each reaching into the hundreds of billions of dollars.

Anthropic and OpenAI depend on state-of-the-art data centers to train models and serve customers, and they're trying to build out capacity as quickly as possible to meet demand. Anthropic said in an amended IPO prospectus this week that it has entered into purchase commitments and lease agreements with an aggregate committed spend of about $895.4 billion, "substantially all of which is related to compute and infrastructure."

Americans grappling with inflation and stubbornly high gas prices have grown worried that the facilities could exacerbate environmental harms, raise utility bills and contribute to job loss. For many, a proposed data center in their backyard became the physical manifestation of the widespread antipathy toward AI.

In the risk factors section of its prospectus, Anthropic said opposition to data center development has become more organized across jurisdictions, and that the company's public profile could draw particular scrutiny to projects associated with it.

Nicole Greene, an analyst at Gartner, said she expects that Anthropic's executives have long been prepared to contend with AI backlash, especially given the disruptive nature of the technology.

"There's going to be big emotions," Greene told CNBC. "With big emotions and feelings — fear and excitement — comes heightened scrutiny."

Amodei acknowledged the souring sentiment around AI in a post on X in August. He said it's the result of a "crisis of trust," and that people believe companies, the government and the tech industry are always "cooking up some new way to screw them over."

Rebuilding public confidence is going to be an uphill battle as the company begins providing quarterly financial updates.

The University of Southern California published a survey on Monday that found that 71% of respondents don't trust big tech companies to act in society's best interest "at all" around data center development.

"Once you've lost trust, it's hard to gain it back," Wändi Bruine de Bruin, a professor at the University of Southern California's Schaeffer Institute, said in an interview. "The numbers are not good."

#2879. St. Cloud State surrenders five points to Omaha as McLaughlin carted off ice

ST. CLOUD —The St. Cloud State men's hockey team faltered on the road against Omaha, going 0-1-1 on the weekend to fall to 3-5-1 on the season.

In the first game, the Huskies battled back from a one-goal deficit to earn a 2-2 tie against the Mavericks on Friday night at Baxter Arena, but Omaha claimed the NCHC standings points with a 1-0 victory in a shootout following a scoreless overtime period.

The Huskies struck early on the power play when freshman defenseman Will Sharpe scored his first collegiate goal just 1:49 into the contest. Omaha swiftly answered on a goal from Gavin Hodnett before Ryan McCleary paced the Mavs ahead in the second period.

St. Cloud State responded in the third period after generating sustained pressure and capitalizing on another power-play opportunity. Following a 5-on-3 advantage, freshman forward Hayden Paupanekis generated a scoring opportunity, leading to sophomore defenseman Finn McLaughlin burying the tying goal into an open net. The Huskies looked much more polished in the final period, outshooting Omaha 17-6, but could not find the go-ahead goal.

More: Charlie Lurie brings 'the best shot' you'll see to St. Cloud State hockey

More: How William Ahlrik's pride for faceoffs has boosted St. Cloud State hockey

More: Freshman Nolan Stevenson making early statement with St. Cloud State hockey

McLaughlin added an assist for his first career multi-point game while goaltender Yan Shostak continued his strong play in net with 31 saves, highlighted by 17 in the second period, as the Huskies outshot Omaha 44-33 and converted 2-of-3 power-play chances.

In the second game of the road trip, the Huskies continued their power play barrage with three additional PP scores from William Ahlrik, Nolan Roed and Carson Pilgrim, but could not keep up with the Mavs in a 5-3 loss, surrendering five points total on the weekend.

St. Cloud State showed plenty of fight, erasing a two-goal deficit to pull even with Omaha in the third period, but a go-ahead score from Maxime Pellerin on the power play with 3:53 remaining and an empty-netter late handed the Huskies a hard-to-swallow loss. Shostak had 19 saves in the loss.

To make matters worse for the Huskies, in the midst of the best weekend of his collegiate career, McLaughlin was carted off the ice behind the net after a heavy collision.

SCSU Athletics does not have an update on McLaughlin's status at this time.

McLaughlin transferred in from Minnesota, where he played in 31 games as a true freshman. The Canmore, Alberta, native has recorded two goals and three assists this season to pair with 10 blocks.

The Huskies host No. 8 Minnesota Duluth next weekend in what is bound to be another competitive, but difficult weekend for a St. Cloud squad in the midst of gathering its footing in the early part of the season.

#2878. Ultimate Fan: It’s a good time to open up the offense, keep the defensive pressure coming

Ultimate Fans, life could not get better in Broncos Country right now. Denver is coming off an incredible victory over KC, currently holding court at the top of the AFC West, before a short trip out East to play the Panthers in spectacular weather just in front of a much-needed bye week.

AND on top of that, we have a fabulous UFG from another younger fan and newbie to the series who knocked it out the park just like his predecessors.

While Ky Broncos Fan wasn't even alive during the John Elway Era, his momma raised him right and turned him on to the GOAT and early Broncos' Lombardis. Watching the Tebow-to-DT thriller turned him into a fan for life and his Broncos' Mount Rushmore is testament to his appreciation for Denver's incredible history. So that gives you a hint of the treat you are in for!

I'm going to be at this game, and my track record is pretty good, so let's assume it's going to continue!

MHR – First, we have to talk about that KC game. What in the world happened? How excited/nervous were you for those final 15 seconds?Ky Broncos Fan: What happened was some Mile High Magic! I was standing in front of the TV pacing like a coach, and when that obvious false start was called (no matter what Brittany Mahomes thinks), I knew fate was on our side. The defining play of that sequence in my mind was JL Skinner stuffing Walker at the line of scrimmage after being forced into the game due to injuries; that kind of grit and determination is emblematic of the whole team. Last Sunday was my birthday, and the KC game was the perfect gift!

MHR – We used to do a post-game story at Mile High Report asking, "what did you learn about the Broncos this week?" It's a great question, so have YOU learned about the Broncos the last few weeks?Ky Broncos Fan: I've learned that the Broncos are still elite in late game situations. Over and over against some of the best teams in the league (besides the drubbing of the Cardinals), they've proved they still know how to win with their backs against the wall. The Arizona win was a nice reminder that fans can expect the Broncos to beat the teams they're supposed to beat instead of every week feeling like a coin flip.

MHR – The Broncos sit atop the AFC West at 6-2, but they rank 30th in total offense. Bo Nix has looked much better in the wins over Arizona and Kansas City. Has he turned a corner, or is this offense still living on late comebacks? As I've asked many before, is a constant comeback-style game, Ky Broncos Fan: The offense is improving, but the late comebacks are still hiding deeper issues. Establishing the run early will go a long way for more offensive consistency, and luckily for Broncos fans, Jonah Coleman seems more than ready to take that on. As for Bo, we all fell in love with his fire and gunslinging mentality last season when it mattered most. As he continues to regain confidence following the ankle injury, Denver needs him to trust his eyes and take the shots when they're there. His aversion to negative plays is one of his best traits, but Denver is at its best when he's balancing that with more risk-taking.

MHR – Carolina has been without starting corners Jaycee Horn and Mike Jackson, and Akayleb Evans is also on injured reserve. How aggressive should Sean Payton/Davis Webb be with telling Bo Nix to throw downfield on Sunday? Courtland Sutton and Jaylen Waddle have been key to moving the chains but not super explosive…any chance we get a big day from either/both?Ky Broncos Fan: This game is the perfect opportunity to unleash the passing game. Utilize the running backs to get ahead of the chains, and then open up the playbook and test their secondary. Rushing success will open up some play-action opportunities, and overall, I think pushing the ball downfield in this game is necessary to gaining confidence and setting the passing game up for more success later in the season. Waddle's speed and elusiveness are nightmares for opposing defenders, and I think he's overdue for turning a quick slant up field for a long touchdown. I also think we're overdue for a Sutton long bomb TD. When Bo puts it in the right place, Sutton turns 50/50 balls into 80/20 balls, and I want to see him get some chances to convert big plays and gain confidence this week.

MHR – On the other hand, the Panthers' run defense is atrocious (ranked 30th)…is this a game for Jonah Coleman to really break out? After that 51-yard catch-and-run + 5-yard TD last week…maybe he's poised for a big second half finish to the season? How would you suggest using him and JK Dobbins this week?Ky Broncos Fan: I'm starting Jonah Coleman in fantasy again this week. I spent a decent portion of my free agency budget to get him, and the Panthers' defense is the perfect chance for him to show the front office we don't need to spend draft capital on Jonathan Taylor. Dobbins can still be a very effective runner when healthy, and the priority has to be keeping Dobbins healthy for a playoff run. I'd make Coleman the true bellcow and spell him with Dobbins occasionally.

MHR – Nix dismissed the "trap game" talk. But it's a 1 p.m EST kickoff (11 a.m. Mile High time) right after an emotional comeback. Are you concerned the offense comes out flat? How does Nix avoid the offense getting in its own way?Ky Broncos Fan: I'm concerned. As someone who lives in eastern time, the Broncos always seem to start flat when they come to my time zone. Nix can avoid the offense getting in its own way with one word: handoff. Let Coleman and Dobbins do their jobs, and use their success to test the secondary in favorable situations. There are times when the playcalling should favor some early down aggressiveness to put Carolina on their heels too, but those playcalls have to be high-completion percentage opportunities that Payton trusts the offense to execute.

MHR – The Broncos defense has been mostly outstanding, coming up big when it needs to, even if it gives up some plays. How should Vance Joseph plan for Bryce Young, who has been having a decent season getting the ball in the hands of reigning Offensive Rookie of the Year Tetairoa McMillan and Jalen Coker, an equally dangerous receiver?Ky Broncos Fan: The secondary can trust the front line to get home. Young is easily overwhelmed by pressure and possibly dealing with an undisclosed knee injury that is altering his mobility. Joseph doesn't need to blitz more; just changing up coverage looks enough to keep Young guessing will buy the front line the time it needs to make game-changing plays. McMillan and Coker are great young receivers, but Denver's secondary has stymied greater, more experienced players already this season.

MHR – How should Vance Joseph take advantage of a depleted offensive line standing in front of Bryce Young…does the QB even have a chance with Zach Allen, Nik Bonitto, Jonathan Cooper, Malcolm Roach coming at him? With 23 sacks as a team this season, how worried should Young be on Sunday?Ky Broncos Fan: Young should be terrified. Carolina's path to success is a quick short passing game to get the ball out and mitigate the pass rush, but as long as the secondary and linebackers stay disciplined, they should face 3rd and longs routinely that let Allen, Bonitto and the others feast. Young has issues with the ball getting batted down at the line of scrimmage too, and the defense should be able to exploit him and create some chaos. They've made better quarterbacks than Young look worse this year, and I'm excited to see what they can pull off tomorrow.

MHR – Alex Singleton was named AFC Defensive Player of the Month. He's not always the best in coverage, but he does fly to the ball. What kind of impact do you think he has on the defense?Ky Broncos Fan: He's the emotional heart of the defense. His passion and elite tackling fuel everyone else's fires, and while he's not the most agile speedster of a linebacker we've seen, he mostly keeps himself out of bad situations by reading plays well. Carolina should try to isolate him in coverage, but that's easier said than done.

MHR – Denver ranks 25th against the run, allowing 127.9 yards per game. Carolina is coming off a 10-day "mini-bye." Is the run defense a weakness the Panthers can exploit?Ky Broncos Fan: It unfortunately is. Carolina's running backs aren't world-beaters, but a lack of disciplined tackling will allow their run game to take off quickly. I'm no defensive guru, but I'm worried about playoff matchups with better running backs come January if Denver can't clean up their run defense by then.

MHR – We are halfway through the season and just before a bye. Who are your midseason MVPs – offense, defense, ST?Ky Broncos Fan: Offense: Garret Bolles. PFF has him as 3rd best tackle in football this season, and he hasn't given up a sack yet. His growth over his career is amazing, and Denver's lucky to have him. I hope he retires here. Defense: Alex Singleton. Leading the league in tackles is amazing, but time and time again, we see him make big plays when it matters most. ST: Will Lutz. He's a fantastic kicker who seems more accurate in bigger moments. Lutz is exactly the kind of kicker you want on your roster for a postseason run.

Stats for each Broncos RB? Coleman: 20 carries, 87 yards, 2 TD. Dobbins: 6 carries, 15 yards.

Number of yards receiving/TDs for each WR/TE? Sutton: 5 catches, 68 yards, 1 TD. Waddle: 6 catches, 82 yards, 1 TD. Mims: 2 catches, 46 yards. Franklin: 1 catch, 8 yards. Engram: 2 catches, 12 yards. Trautman: 1 catch, 18 yards.

Number of times Nix barely misses getting sacked? 4

Broncos player with the most tackles? Singleton

Who gets the first sack of the game for the Broncos? Allen

Who gets any sacks? Allen, Bonitto, Cooper, Singleton

How many INTs/FF/FR for the Panthers' D? 0 INTs, 1 FF, 1 FR.

How many INTs/FF/FR for the Broncos' D? 1 INT, 2 FF, 1 FR.

How many completely wrong DPI penalties called Sunday? 2 on Moss, 0 on others.

Favorite John Elway win outside of the Super Bowls? The Drive, I can't rewatch it without getting chills.

Favorite Broncos win ever? Super Bowl 50. This one's for Pat!

Favorite Peyton Manning win? His 7-TD decimation of the Ravens to open 2013.

Toughest Peyton Manning loss? The Super Bowl 48 beat down. I couldn't dream up a worse ending for the best offense in the history of the league. That it gave a young QB named Russell Wilson a Super Bowl ring and contributed to Denver selling the farm for him years later is a cruel twist of fate.

Team you love to beat the most in the AFC West? The Chiefs. When the Raiders are relevant again it will be them.

Coach you laugh at the most in the NFL? Brian Schottenheimer. It's Jerry's world, Brian's just living in it.

Coach you most want to "bench?" Zac Taylor. Joe Burrow and that roster with a better head coach would turn some heads. Burrow has helped him keep his job, but Taylor is holding the team back.

Team you hate to lose to the most in the rest of the AFC? The Patriots.

NFC team you could cheer for in the Super Bowl? The Giants. John Harbaugh is a first class coach and human being. My Baltimore-native wife wishes they hadn't run him off to New York!

NFC team you want to face more than any other in the Super Bowl? The 49ers. A rematch with them and beating a Shanahan for Denver's 4th championship would be poetic.

Favorite game looking forward to this season? The Bills on Christmas Day.

Easiest game left on the schedule? Week 18 against the Chargers.

Toughest game left on the schedule? Week 17 at New England, but thank goodness the Bills are in Denver.

Favorite Broncos player on the current roster? Pat Surtain II.

Favorite Broncos player of all time not named John Elway? Demaryius Thomas.

Favorite new guy/rookie on the team? Jonah Coleman.

Food/Snack you have to eat on game day? Popcorn.

Favorite game analyst/commentator? Mike Tirico is always excellent.

If YOU were choosing your Broncos' Mt. Rushmore, who would be included? Floyd Little, John Elway, Peyton Manning, Randy Gradishar.

How did you become a Broncos fan? I didn't grow up particularly close to any NFL teams, but my mom had liked the Broncos since the 90s due to Elway. The first season I remember paying close attention to was the Tebow year (I was born in 2000). I vividly remember watching the entire Broncos-Steelers playoff game, and as I watched Demaryius Thomas fly down the sideline as Mile High erupted, I fell in love with the Broncos and have watched every game religiously ever since.

#2875. Three Billy Edwards TD passes lift UNC to 38-21 win over UConn in Belichick’s return to New England

Billy Edwards threw for three touchdowns and North Carolina scored 21 points off three turnovers as the Tar Heels defeated UConn 38-21 to snap a two-game losing streak in coach Bill Belichick's return to New England.

It was Belichick's first game back coaching in New England since his tenure with the NFL's Patriots came to an end.

"It's good to be back in New England," Belichick said. "It was an interesting game. I think UConn is a really challenging team, they put a lot of pressure on us.

"The team rebounded pretty well after a couple of tough losses."

Timir Hickman-Collins returned an interception 62 yards for a touchdown and had a fumble recovery to set up a 6-yard touchdown pass from Edwards to Jordan Shipp. Edwards had a fourth-down completion and put the game away with a 3-yard scoring pass to Jelani Thurman with 3:38 remaining after Jalon Thompson's interception gave the ball back to the Tar Heels.

"We've been really harping on (getting) turnovers, every day in practice we have been doing that," Hickman-Collins said. "Today, me and our DB (Jalon Thompson), a lot of us had turnovers."

Kalieb Osborne had completions of 23 and 28 yards and a 25-yard run to set up Cyncir Bowers' 13-yard touchdown run to pull UConn within seven points with 10:01 remaining in the third quarter. Osborne threw for 245 yards, but completed just 17 of 35 passes against a North Carolina defense that was allowing teams to complete 65% of their passes.

"I thought Carolina put us in some tough situations and won the football game," UConn coach Jason Candle said. "I didn't feel like we were at our best. We did some things that set them up in short fields. The turnovers were a big deal today."

Benjamin Hall had a 39-yard touchdown run. He finished with 81 yards on nine carries. Edwards connected with Jelani Thurman on a 3-yard touchdown with 24 seconds remaining in the first half. Thurman now has eight touchdown catches to tie the North Carolina single-season record for TDs by a tight end.

Osborne threw touchdown passes of 18 yards to Ryder Treadway and 24 yards to Cam Abshire as the Huskies trailed 28-14 at halftime.

Peyton Seelmann led North Carolina (5-4) with nine tackles and two sacks. Hayden Pegg had 12 tackles and an interception for UConn (4-5).

Belichick coached the Patriots to six Super Bowl titles. He played at Wesleyan University from 1971-74, which is about 20 miles away from Rentschler Field. Belichick nearly guided North Carolina to a win over No. 1 Miami last week.

Bob Diaco, North Carolina's defensive line coach, coached UConn from 2014-16. UConn won six games in Diaco's second season before losing to Marshall in the St. Petersburg Bowl. He was fired after a 3-9 record in 2016. Diaco went 11-26 in his three seasons with the Huskies.

North Carolina hosts Louisville on Nov. 14.

UConn is home against James Madison on Nov. 14.

#2873. Observations after Sixers suffer blowout loss to Cavs, fall in NBA Cup

Observations after Sixers suffer blowout loss to Cavs, fall in NBA Cup originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers couldn't rekindle the magic of their comeback home-opening win over the Cavaliers.

They fell Friday night in NBA Cup action, suffering a 119-95 defeat in Cleveland. The Sixers dipped to 8-2 and the Cavs improved to 5-4.

The Sixers' top scorers were Jaylen Brown (20 points), VJ Edgecombe (19 points) and Tyrese Maxey (17).

The Cavs got 23 points from Donovan Mitchell and 22 from Evan Mobley.

The Sixers' injured players included center Adem Bona (cervical spine sprain). Cleveland was down big man Jarrett Allen (right knee contusion). Sam Merrill sat out the second half with a right elbow injury.

With Friday's loss, the Sixers are 1-1 in Eastern Conference Group B of the NBA Cup. Their next Cup game is scheduled for Nov. 25 against the Heat.

The Sixers will conclude their three-road trip Sunday afternoon against the Bulls. Here are observations on their blowout loss to Cleveland:

After a missed Merrill three-pointer, the Sixers steamed ahead on the fast break and Edgecombe opened the scoring with a slam.

The Cavs replied with a 15-0 run kickstarted by six straight Mitchell points. Much like in the Sixers' Oct. 22 win over Cleveland, they were many levels below their best in the first quarter.

The Sixers began 0 for 4 from three-point range, including two LeBron James misses. The Cavs were also the better team in transition and limited the Sixers' chances to attack in the open floor by taking excellent care of the ball in the early going. Edgecombe was called for his second foul with 7:34 left in the first quarter.

Meanwhile, Joel Embiid expressed his frustration about a couple of early no-calls. Embiid's larger problem was that he couldn't hit any sort of shot besides free throws.

He went 0 for 9 from the floor in the game. The seven-time All-Star finished with six points and seven rebounds.

Embiid has had a subpar shooting start to the year, especially from three-point territory — he's now 3 for 28 (10.7 percent) — but Friday was the kind of extreme night that's bound to happen here and there over the course of a long season.

Of course, it's notable that Embiid's been playing through right foot plantar fasciitis and the Sixers have been keeping a close eye on his minutes. We'll see how the team manages his availability moving forward and whether Embiid is able to bounce back next time out.

James didn't play anywhere near his finest game either. He had eight points on 3-for-12 shooting, eight rebounds, three assists and six turnovers.

As a team, the Sixers were 36.5 percent from the field and 25 percent beyond the arc.

Jabari Walker rejoined the Sixers' rotation as the backup center with Bona sidelined. Anfernee Simons, former Cav Dean Wade and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope all remained rotation fixtures.

With Walker and Caldwell-Pope on the court next to three starters, the Sixers cut their deficit to single digits in the second quarter. Walker threw down a fast-break dunk that cut Cleveland's lead to 48-41. He gave the Sixers some good defensive possessions when switched on to James Harden and provided his usual physical, high-effort work on the glass.

Edgecombe was fantastic in the second, sinking three triples, racking up 15 points and nabbing three steals.

Many second-year players would have a tough time finding a rhythm after encountering early foul trouble, but Edgecombe was great as soon as he checked back in and delivered a ton of valuable buckets in the Sixers' comeback push.

The Sixers pulled to within 66-63 when Maxey picked a steal and threw a pretty one-handed bounce pass to Edgecombe for a dunk.

With Embiid and James offering little as scorers, Brown hit several shots that helped the Sixers stay in the game. After his 30-point performance last time out against the Pistons, Brown reached the 20-point mark for a third consecutive game.

Still, the Sixers' offense never got rolling. The Cavs maintained their lead and extended it late in the third quarter. By early in the fourth, the Sixers trailed by over 20 points.

Embiid didn't play the fourth quarter and logged 25 minutes. He's had zero made field goals on just one other occasion in his career, back in Nov. 2019 against the Raptors.

Even with their tremendous first nine games, the Sixers knew they'd hit speed bumps throughout the year. They'll try to get back on track Sunday in Chicago.

#2869. Notre Dame knocks off Miami. Fans storm field after Fighting Irish win

Notre Dame entered Saturday needing a statement win over Miami to strengthen its College Football Playoff resume after being left out of last season's 12-team field because of a head-to-head tiebreaker loss to the Hurricanes.

The Fighting Irish held off a late Miami rally, getting a game-clinching stop and takeaway on the Hurricanes' final possession to secure a 31-24 win at Notre Dame Stadium on Saturday, Nov. 7. As Jaiden Ausberry recovered a Leonard Moore-forced fumble to end the game, fans stormed the field to celebrate Notre Dame's biggest victory of the season.

#2868. What the Packers’ roster could look like vs. the Patriots

The Green Bay Packers only made one roster move on Saturday, bringing up practice squad tight end Drake Dabney to the game-day roster for action against the New England Patriots. As always, the moves that the Packers make, and don't make, give us hints at what the roster is expected to look like on Sunday. Let's get into the information we have now.

Four players have already been ruled out with injuries, bringing the Packers' 53-man active roster to 49. The players out are tight end Josh Whyle (concussion), offensive lineman Travis Glover (knee), offensive lineman Sean Rhyan (ankle) and linebacker Zaire Franklin (shin).

The Packers lost Whyle in-game against the Carolina Panthers, carrying only three tight ends on game day and scratching Jonnu Smith for the first time this season, while Glover, Rhyan and Franklin did not play.

This season, the Packers have seemingly had no interest in carrying four running backs on the game-day roster. The same can be said about a ninth offensive lineman, despite the team consistently keeping 11 on the active roster.

If you add in those scratches, Green Bay's active roster drops down to 47 players. With the addition of Dabney, who is expected to replace the special teams snaps of Whyle (the only real special teams contributor on offense who isn't a receiver), that gets us to a 48-man gameday roster. It's pretty tidy.

The players listed as questionable going into the weekend were running back MarShawn Lloyd (knee), cornerback Keisean Nixon (groin) and running back Chris Brooks (ankle). Brooks isn't on the active roster yet, as he's going through his practice window after spending time on the injured reserve. If he were going to play on Sunday, you would have seen an activation today.

Nixon is highly likely to play, as the Packers don't really have a replacement for him on the roster, as it stands currently. If he were going to miss this game, we probably would have seen a second call-up from the practice squad on Saturday. Lloyd may or may not play. If he can't go, the team will displace him with Kaleb Johnson, Green Bay's fourth running back, who has been a healthy scratch since Josh Jacobs' return to the team.

Here's what the team will probably look like on Sunday (with the biggest questions being which offensive lineman gets healthy-scratched and if Lloyd can go):

WR: Christian Watson, Matthew Golden, Savion Williams, Bo Melton, J. Michael Sturdivant and Coleman Owen

TE: Tucker Kraft, Mark Redman, Jonnu Smith and Drake Dabney

OL: 8 players up, 3 scratched (two for sure being Sean Rhyan and Travis Glover)

Most likely: Jordan Morgan, Aaron Banks, John Williams, Laken Tomlinson, Anthony Belton, Kevin Zeitler, Mekhi Becton Sr. and Jager Burton active; Donnovan Jennings a healthy scratch, along with Rhyan and Glover being out with injury.

DL: Karl Brooks, Devonte Wyatt, Chris McClellan, Jordon Riley and Anthony Campbell

ED: Lukas Van Ness, Barryn Sorrell, Brenton Cox Jr. and Dani Dennis-Sutton

LB: Isaiah McDuffie, Ty'Ron Hopper, Nick Niemann and Kristian Welch

CB: Keisean Nixon, Brandon Cisse, Carrington Valentine, Benjamin St-Juste and Kamal Hadden

S: Xavier McKinney, Evan Williams, Javon Bullard, Kitan Oladapo

That's your 48-man roster against the Patriots.
