#2867. What we learned as Warriors' streak reaches seven games with OT win vs. Magic

What we learned as Warriors' streak reaches seven games with OT win vs. Magic originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

SAN FRANCISCO – Winning six straight games is hard enough. Extending their win streak to seven straight looked like a walk in the park for the Warriors on Saturday night, until it wasn't.

The Warriors started off extremely strong, just to watch a 16-point lead fade away. Contenders need to rise to the challenge and overcome adversity, and that's what the Warriors did in a 126-119 overtime win over the Orlando Magic at Chase Center.

Beating the Magic gave the Warriors their first seven-game win streak without Jimmy Butler since January/February of their 2022 NBA championship season.

Steph Curry was incredible in the fourth quarter and overtime, as was rookie Yaxel Lendeborg.

The elder Curry scored a game-high 41 points on 13-of-19 shooting, going 5 of 10 from 3-point range and 10 of 10 at the free-throw line. Lendeborg added 23 points on 9-of-15 shooting and had eight rebounds.

A revitalized Strength In Numbers bench continued to be huge for the Warriors, outscoring the Magic 42 to 31.

There will be a lot to clean watching the film, but the Warriors will take a gritty win in the end.

Here are three takeaways from the Warriors improving to 7-3 on the season.

Winning time is where superstars are made. There's nobody in the building that doubts Curry in the clutch. But every hero needs a co-star alongside him. For Curry, that's a rookie wearing a cape.

The fourth quarter was won on the backs of a 38-year-old Curry and a 24-year-old Lendeborg in his 10th career game.

In the first three quarters, Lendeborg scored seven points and was a bit of an afterthought. Then in the fourth quarter, Lendeborg utilized his size and skill set to score nine huge points on a perfect 4-of-4 shooting. But this was another Steph Show for everyone to sit back and enjoy his 14 fourth-quarter points.

Between the two, Curry and Lendeborg combined to score 23 points in the fourth quarter on 8-of-9 shooting. Curry was on the bench when overtime began, coming in at the 3:48 mark with the Warriors ahead by two points. He and Lendeborg scored 12 of the Warriors' 16 points in overtime to claim victory.

Winning the margins is a major talking point of Warriors coach Steve Kerr to have success. His team needs to take care of the ball, focus on hitting singles instead of home runs and win the possession battle. The Warriors didn't execute those key parts of the game against a shorthanded Magic team that seemed to grab every loose ball.

Let's start with turnovers. As the first quarter crept to a close, the Warriors began to get loose with the ball. And the theme continued in the wrong ways for the home team. By halftime, even with the Warriors enjoying a 16-point lead, they already had given up 10 turnovers for 14 points.

As the Warriors' lead evaporated in the third quarter, they kept counting turnovers and were up to 14 for 18 points going into the fourth. But the Warriors only had one turnover between the fourth quarter and overtime, giving the ball away a total of 19 times for 26 points.

Another major area of concern was on the offensive glass. The Magic continued to keep possessions alive, and it finally caught up to the Warriors. While the Warriors came down with a lowly three offensive rebounds, the Magic secured a ridiculous 24, which helped lead to 33 second-chance points compared to the Warriors' two.

However he can, Gui Santos wants to help the Warriors win games. He was pushing through a left knee injury that held him back, and finally had to be told to take a seat. Santos missed the previous two games, both Warriors wins, before returning Saturday night.

He was back in the starting lineup, but couldn't give the Warriors much. At all.

Santos played just 11-plus minutes and didn't have much of an impact. Playing less than he usually would, Santos had more turnovers (two) than shots made (one). That's in a game where he has just one rebound and no assists.

Defensively, Santos didn't have much burst either. It's clear he's less than 100 percent, and the Warriors will need a much better version of him soon. Santos finished as a minus-4 with three points and didn't leave the bench in the fourth quarter or overtime.

#2865. Binance breach puts custody at center of crypto infrastructure trade

The $6.3 billion Binance hack could accelerate a shift in how investors value cryptocurrency companies, putting a greater premium on custody providers and the infrastructure needed to securely hold and protect digital assets.

Much of crypto's evolution this year has involved companies moving past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, to focus instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance. Last Thursday's breach adds a new dimension, bringing cybersecurity, custody, compliance and balance-sheet resilience into sharper focus.

Importantly, the Binance breach was not an indictment of crypto itself, or a case of leadership-driven fraud, as with FTX in late 2022. Instead, Binance's leadership moved quickly to contain the fallout, protect customers and commit the exchange's own resources to cover losses.

On Tuesday, Binance CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that the company has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Management's response, alongside efforts elsewhere in the industry to boost confidence, helped prevent a security breach from escalating into a broader crisis of confidence. For example, Tether, Circle, Ripple and others moved quickly to freeze or restrict access to about $3 billion in stolen assets; Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

The hack could even help shape the next phase of the crypto infrastructure trade. The new winners may not just be the businesses with the most users, volume or assets under custody, but the ones that can also demonstrate they're able to protect those assets under extreme duress.

"Which company has the resources to invest in cybersecurity and protect customer funds? For a smaller company, it could be much harder because once these crypto companies become infrastructure plays, providing the well for tokenization … they need to have the compliance and control in place so that a similar hacking will not happen or can be minimized," Owen Lau, senior analyst at ClearStreet, told CNBC. "Larger companies have more resources that could give them some advantage."

While institutional investors have increasingly embraced digital assets, broader adoption depends on confidence that those assets can be securely held, accessed and transferred. The Binance breach highlights the importance of that infrastructure, and could increase demand for specialized custodians and platforms with established asset-protection capabilities.

Coinbase has operated a dedicated institutional custody business since 2018. Circle entered the custody business this year, after the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency granted it approval to operate as a trust bank. In the past few years, Ripple and Kraken have also launched custody businesses.

Still, smaller players compete in the space as well. Small cap crypto exchange stock Gemini Space Station has offered custody services since 2019. BitGo Holdings, founded in 2013, is one of the earliest players in institutional crypto custody and infrastructure, with a reputation for security and serving major institutions.

The shift could also drive consolidation as companies look to buy capabilities, deepen their infrastructure offerings and achieve the scale needed to compete, Lau said.

The more consequential structural shift may be the separation of custody from execution, according to Morgan Stanley.

"Crypto exchanges have historically combined trading, custody, and settlement within vertically integrated platforms, concentrating operational risk. Repeated security breaches could encourage investors to move assets toward independent custody while continuing to trade on CEXs," the firm's analyst Felix Stratmann said in a note Monday," referring to centralized cryptocurrency exchanges.

"This would preserve the role of centralized exchanges as liquidity and execution venues, while potentially benefiting regulated custodians, including banks, as institutional investors place greater emphasis on asset segregation, operational resilience, and investor protection," Stratmann said.

#2860. What Josh Heupel said about officials call on Tennessee's DaSaahn Brame in end zone

Tennessee football coach Josh Heupel got an unsatisfying explanation from officials on a key pass into the end zone during the Vols win over Kentucky at Neyland Stadium on Nov. 7.

With the Vols struggling to find consistency in the passing game, and nursing a four-point lead over the Wildcats, quarterback George MacIntyre fired a pass for tight end DaSaahn Brame into the far corner of the end zone. The pass was ruled incomplete, but replay showed that Brame had two hands on the ball and got at least one foot down.

Tennessee coach Josh Heupel said officials did review the play but didn't agree that Brame met the criteria for a touchdown.

"They said that they did review it," Heupel said postgame. "I asked them."

No. 16 Tennessee scored 21 unanswered points in the fourth quarter to secure a blowout, 34-6 victory over the No. 7 Wildcats and make the controversial call a moot point.

Brame suffered an injury on the play, hitting the back of his head on the turf. He was helped off the field and did not return to the game, finishing with one reception for three yards.

#2849. Democratic sweep puts crypto on defensive in Washington

Democrats' sweep of Congress likely resets the political calculus for crypto, ushering in a more adversarial approach in Washington and pushing industry-friendly legislation like the Clarity Act to the back burner.

With crypto critics like Sherrod Brown, D-Ohio, returning to the Senate and Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., retaining significant influence over financial policy, analysts say the new Congress is likely to focus on scrutinizing President Donald Trump's personal crypto dealings over supportive industry legislation.

For years, crypto fought an uphill battle in Washington, asking for clear operating rules, but often landing on the receiving end of regulation by enforcement. That shifted after Trump's 2024 election victory, giving the industry a reprieve and seat at the policy table.

"We see Sherrod Brown winning in Ohio as damaging the crypto industry's political clout as the industry worked to defeat him," Jaret Seiberg, TD Cowen's Washington analyst, said in a note Wednesday. "We did not think Clarity could advance over the next two years. This makes us more confident in our prediction."

The crypto industry spent more than $200 million to sway the midterms, hoping to get leaders elected that could push a comprehensive crypto market structure bill over the finish line after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate.

Seiberg emphasized that Democrats "will investigate Trump's crypto entanglements including World Liberty Financial" and said "the resulting political environment may make it impossible to advance a version of Clarity that the crypto sector could back."

Mike Townsend, Charles Schwab's managing director of legislative and regulatory affairs, said he expects "Democrats to push next year for a version of the bill that is much tougher on cryptocurrency companies."

The stakes extend beyond market structure legislation via Clarity. Stablecoin innovation; decentralized finance, or DeFi, development; banking access; tokenization; and sensible tax policy are priorities the industry sees as important to ensuring the U.S. remains competitive in the global crypto economy.

The industry will need continued bipartisan engagement to ensure these priorities aren't sidelined by changing political dynamics. And Democrats have historically been less supportive of crypto than Republicans, though that divide has narrowed as more Democrats embrace the industry.

The implications of Democrats' big win aren't all bad. Crypto has reached a level of financial integration that could be difficult to reverse, with its future increasingly centered on establishing crypto as financial infrastructure. Even with the Clarity Act potentially stalled until 2028, key financial regulators can help advance the industry through rule-making.

Coinbase's vice president of U.S. policy, Kara Calvert, points out that the election results point to a historic shift in the makeup of the incoming Congress, with a record number of pro-crypto voices expected to join its ranks.

"With last night's results, the 120th Congress is on track to have more new pro-crypto members than we've ever seen in any freshman class," she said. "Crypto is 100% bipartisan, and that's why the future of finance does not rest on either political party or a single election night. Democrats and Republicans alike know that clear rules protect consumers and keep innovation in the U.S."

As of Thursday morning, 19 candidates characterized as pro crypto by the advocacy nonprofit Stand With Crypto have been elected to the Senate versus eight Senate candidates it characterizes as anti crypto. In the House, 228 pro-crypto candidates have been elected versus 106 the group deems anti crypto.

Several races have not been called, but Fairshake — the super PAC launched in 2023 to support pro-crypto candidates regardless of party — has so far won 29 of the 34 races it spent on, per Reuters, including three of the six House candidates it prioritized by spending a baseline of $1 million on their campaigns.

Dante Disparte, chief strategy officer and head of global policy and operations at Circle, emphasized Circle's bipartisan approach and readiness for new Senate and House leadership, but said it "may take longer to get Clarity tabled again."

That bipartisan support may offer a blueprint for what comes next, but even if crypto legislation stalls, progress can continue, said Joseph Chalom, CEO of ether treasury company Sharplink.

"We expect the Clarity Act to remain on hold through 2028. Even so, there is a clear path forward for stablecoins … and for the tokenization of public equities under the SEC's five-year innovation exemption," he said. "Technological progress is rarely rolled back. Once the benefits of faster settlement, lower costs and markets that never close become clear to investors and institutions, this shift will be very difficult to reverse, regardless of who wins the White House in 2028."

Near-term, investors should expect more volatility as the market tries to figure out how crypto regulation may shift under a Democratic Congress.

The impact of the blue wave "should vary considerably across assets," said James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares. "We would expect U.S. crypto equities to be more sensitive than bitcoin, particularly businesses reliant on new regulatory permissions. Established, compliant operators could even benefit from greater scrutiny of competitors. So miners who already have data centers set up are likely to remain in high demand by AI hyperscalers."

— CNBC's Talia Kaplan contributed reporting.

#2848. Three things I’m looking at in the New York Jets vs. Kansas City Chiefs Game

We're back ready to watch our New York Jets play against the Kansas City Chiefs. For the third straight week, the Jets are facing a team that's coming off consecutive losses and looking to "get right" against Gang Green. Miami and Las Vegas were able to accomplish that feat and it seems like Kansas City should theoretically have no problem doing the same. The Chiefs started the season 5-0 and although they've been inconsistent, they are coached by one of the greatest coaches in history, have a great defense and a first ballot hall of famer at QB.

It'll be quite the tall task for the Jets to upset the Chiefs. While the Jets were actually the favorites going into the last two weeks at home, they are a 9.5-point underdog as they enter Arrowhead Stadium. It's tied with the Raiders-Niners game for the largest spread in the NFL this week so that should help paint the picture of how tough this will be for the Jets to overcome. Still, anything is possible and this year's Jets have firepower! Let's take a look:

I usually pride myself on discussing the "lesser-talked" items that don't get mainstream media attention, but the elephant in the Jets' RB room is too massive to ignore. The entire fanbase and media have completely turned on Breece Hall (again) and have spent the last month hypothetically trading him all over the league. As the sole human being left who still wants him to spend his entire 20's as a Jet, this game seems like a real pivotal moment for that dream I have.

Let's make no mistake about it: Braelon Allen has been absolutely incredible these last four weeks. He has arguably been the best rusher in the entire NFL during this time, averaging over 100 yards per game and scoring the game-winning touchdown against the Browns. He's proven to be a valuable rushing asset, which was very much up for debate before he suited up against the Bears this season.

The question is, is there enough pie for multiple running backs to eat? Plenty of teams thrive with duos that eat consistently on a weekly basis. The Rams' Kyren Williams and Blake Corum, the Bears' D'Andre Swift and Kyle Monangai, and Broncos' J.K. Dobbins and R.J. Harvey are some quick examples. Even the Raiders who just beat the Jets last week had Mike Washington take 25 carries and Ashton Jeanty still finish the game with 70+ scrimmage yards.

It's up to Frank Reich to deploy these two Killer Bs in a way that maximizes both players' talent, and I think it's easier to do than my colleagues and fellow Jets content creators have claimed. I think there is plenty to go around and wholeheartedly believe it'd be a grave mistake to trade Breece Hall. Of course, conventional wisdom says to keep Braelon running his 17+ carries per game and then utilize Breece's route running and hands to be a weapon in the pass game.

I agree with that general philosophy, but would still value Breece's rushing ability. His issues have been between the tackles but he's explosive in space and I'd utilize those young tackles to deploy outside runs to get Breece going. Luckily, this game will be away, so the crowd won't be a factor – I believe the impatient short-sighted fans have already made their decision so if Hall were to start slow he'd hear his own fans booing him. At Arrowhead, this is a nonfactor, he has the freedom to get himself involved without hearing from the peanut gallery and a chance to prove they can be a duo for years to come.

What does success look like? The Chiefs have allowed 100+ scrimmage to opposing RB1s in three of the last four weeks. That one exception is the Chargers, who are still winless and have been horrible all year. I'm looking for 100+ scrimmage yards for RB1 and 50+ scrimmage yards for RB2. Now, who is RB1 and RB2? That's up for Frank Reich, Breece Hall, and Braelon Allen to decide.

I think we can all agree that Aaron Glenn's superpowers of defensive play-calling are the worst 'powers' out there and that the Jets defense has been an issue. It looks like things are going to get bad to worse because the Chiefs are looking to get their high-powered offense back on track. They were averaging 28 points and undefeated until this two-game skid hit and they scored 27 points combined in these last two.

Now they're facing a Jets team that has allowed 30.6(!) points per game during this three game losing streak. They've especially been leaky to the the running back position, which is horrible news when you consider Kenneth Walker is having a career year. Only Jahmyr Gibbs has more rushing yards per game and only Jaylen Warren has more yards per carry.

They also having a rookie running back Emmett Johnson who just caught five passes for 128(!) yards last week and a touchdown last week. Will this running back duo show us what a real duo looks like and run wild on the porous Jets defense this week? What adjustments can Glenn make, what players can step up, or will it just be "Jets beating Jets" like Minkah Fitzpatrick said last week?

Garrett Wilson is set to miss his first game of the season with a concussion, and while that normally meant complete despair for the offense in the past, there are lots of options to step up this Sunday. Of course Adonai Mitchell and Omar Cooper Jr. seem like the natural choices, but there are cases to be made for Mason Taylor, Kenyon Sadiq and Isaiah Williams. Even the aforementioned Breece Hall can get a lot of looks in the pass game.

Wilson had 12 targets last week. You may be thinking "how do we replace all those targets?" but in the weeks prior you may recall that his targets have been an issue. Geno has had no problem spreading the ball around all season so I'd imagine this week will be more of the same. The most interesting thing to me is seeing who garners the most attention from the Chiefs defense.

If you were Steve Spagnuolo what would you do? Mitchell is coming off a 142-yard game but Omar Cooper Jr. had consecutive 100 yard games before that. With attention and potential double teams occurring on the outside is this a game for the tight ends to finally feast? Sadiq still only has 5 catches for 43 scoreless yards in the five games since his 100-yard breakout performance against the Lions. Hopefully Frank Reich and Geno Smith can keep the offensive momentum rolling without their best WR on the field. Let's go Jets!

#2846. College football rankings: What Notre Dame's win over Miami means for CFP

College football rankings: What Notre Dame's win over Miami means for CFP originally appeared on The Sporting News. Add The Sporting News as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

All year, college football fans have been waiting for Week 10, a slate circled on calendars by many. With a collection of elite matchups, the CFP race got some significant shake-ups.

While Utah, Alabama and Texas avoided any chaotic scenarios by claiming wins and keeping their momentum going toward the playoff, Miami, Kentucky and BYU face more questions after all losing on Saturday.

One way or another, Notre Dame vs. Miami was bound to have lasting CFP consequences — and the Irish's 31-24 win will certainly result in some changes from the committee this week.

Here's a look at how Notre Dame beat Miami and an updated look at the CFP race after the significant Week 10 slate.

MORE: Updated look at Big Ten standings after Oregon, UCLA wins

From the first whistle on Saturday night in South Bend, Notre Dame set the tone in front of its home fans on both sides of the ball.

The Irish defense stifled Darian Mensah and the Hurricanes' offense in the first half, bringing pressureand leaving Mensah scrambling in the absence of star receiver Malachi Toney. Meanwhile, CJ Carr had one of the best performances of his career, especially given the circumstances.

Carr had two touchdown passes in the second quarter, then added another in the third quarter as Notre Dame began to rout Miami.

However, things got interesting late. Mensah came storming back, throwing three touchdown passes himself in the late quarters to cut the Notre Dame deficit to one score on multiple occasions. But on a massive fourth-down play, what appeared to be a touchdown catch for Elija Lofton was instead broken up at the last second, leaving the Irish to drain most of the clock and hold on for an upset win.

MORE:Breaking down Oregon's path to CFP after upset win over Buckeyes

Of the projected CFP teams from last week's rankings, four lost, three of which were losses to fellow projected playoff teams.

Here's a look at how Week 10 went for the previously projected playoff teams, and which teams could fall out of the projected bracket this coming Tuesday.

Down went the Hurricanes. In terms of Miami's CFP outlook, it will be fine, assuming the team closes out the year with wins over Duke, Virginia Tech and Boston College. The Hurricanes are in position to win the ACC title, and even if they don't, they've been dominant enough this season to deserve consideration with one regular-season loss.

Barring a massive collapse, Miami will still be playing in the CFP this season — but it may not be with a first-round bye as anticipated.

Until another team proves otherwise, Georgia is looking more like the favorite to be the No. 1 seed in the CFP. The Bulldogs handed it to Ole Miss in Week 10, and unless Missouri or South Carolina can pull off a major upset in the next two weeks, Kirby Smart's team will likely be playing in the SEC Championship.

Nothing's guaranteed yet, but Georgia should take over as the No. 1-ranked team by the committee with Miami's loss. The only blemish on the Bulldogs' resume is a three-point loss to Auburn.

The Utes are tracking to make their first-ever College Football Playoff. They got their biggest win of the season yet, taking down BYU 28-20 on Saturday to remain unbeaten.

Utah is easily in position to play in the Big 12 title game, and even if it lost that game, a one-loss Utes team may wind up having a strong enough case for an at-large bid anyway.

Curt Cignetti's team was sitting pretty during a wild Week 10 slate in college football. But coming off a bye week, Indiana has a huge game against USC awaiting next week.

The Hoosiers likely won't move much, if at all, in the rankings, but they need a win over the Trojans to stay in position to take on UCLA in the Big Ten Championship. If not, the committee may not be very impressed by a two-loss Indiana team that did not beat a single ranked opponent.

As it turned out, there wasn't much reason to worry about the Crimson Tide in Week 10. They likely ended LSU's playoff chances with a thorough, 31-13 win on the road, a much-needed second win over a ranked opponent.

Assuming Alabama can close out the season with wins over Vanderbilt, Chattanooga and Auburn, not only will the Crimson Tide be in the mix for an SEC Championship Game spot, but they're looking safer for an at-large selection.

Will Stein and the Wildcats have been riding pretty high this season, but their momentum came to a halt in Week 10, handled with ease by Tennessee. With matchups against Florida and Missouri remaining, it's no guarantee Kentucky can still make the CFP.

The Wildcats will drop in the rankings — and Tennessee should be entering the picture — but if Kentucky wins out, it should still be in a solid spot for an at-large bid.

The Longhorns may be a win over LSU away from surviving their up-and-down season to still make the playoff. Texas' two-score win over Missouri was its third-straight, and with wins over Ohio State, Tennessee and Ole Miss, this is a team with a big-time resume for the committee.

The best thing Texas can do is win out, and it will be in a great spot. One more loss could make things a bit too murky to make the playoff, though.

Bob Chesney's unit still couldn't be stopped, downing Minnesota in Week 10. The path to the playoff is only getting simpler; should the Bruins beat Illinois and Michigan over the next two weeks, a matchup against USC is the only real test remaining.

UCLA is in prime position to make the Big Ten Championship, and even a one-loss Bruins team should be enough to make the bracket.

One piece of fortunate news for BYU is that it has a light rest-of-season schedule, hoping to surpass Texas Tech in the conference standings for an opportunity to face Utah again in the Big 12 Championship.

Even if the Cougars can't reach that matchup, assuming they win out vs. Baylor, Kansas and Cincinnati, they should have a decent argument to make the bracket thanks to their Oct. 17 win over Notre Dame.

The other piece of fortunate news for BYU is that two of the lingering projected non-playoff teams in Week 10, Ohio State and LSU, both suffered their third losses of the season, essentially ending their respective cases for an at-large bid. As long as they handle business over the coming weeks, the Cougars should still be in a fine spot.

The Rebels are in trouble — their CFP chances took a massive hit in Week 10, and they may not be able to recover.

Three SEC losses means Ole Miss won't be vying for the conference title game, and no three-loss team has ever made the bracket, either. Considering Ole Miss didn't even look competitive against Georgia, the committee isn't likely to do the Rebels any favors, even if they win out against Oklahoma, Wofford and Mississippi State.

Ole Miss' projected CFP spot will likely be handed over to Tennessee this week.

Prepare to see the Irish make a big jump in the rankings. Notre Dame looked dominant in the first half against Miami on Saturday, and assuming Marcus Freeman's team finishes the season with three more wins, it will be 11-1, which will be plenty safe to make the bracket.

Saturday's win all but locked in Notre Dame's playoff spot, assuming they get the job done over the next few weeks. The Irish get an automatic bid if they finish within the top-12 ranked teams, and they should be in the top-7, at least, after their Week 10 win.

And of course, the Broncos remain in the driver's seat to claim the Group of 6 automatic bid this year. Boise State grabbed its eight-straight win since its season-opening loss to Oregon, dropping 64 points against Colorado State.

As the lone G6 team to be ranked by the committee last week, at this point, it'd be a surprise if a non-Broncos team winds up claiming the No. 12 seed this year.

#2842. CNBC's experts on whether the AI trade is ending and what investors should do next

Amid a powerful rout in tech stocks, investors told CNBC on Thursday that something may be fundamentally breaking in the AI trade following the big success of Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections and the threat they try to slow the data center buildout.

Democrats are more aggressively anti-data center than Republicans, and Wall Street is quickly redoing its math what any restrictions they implement could mean for the related companies' future growth.

"Right now, investors are questioning that $1.2 trillion of capex that we forecast for next year," Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners, told CNBC. "Well, maybe that's not going to happen."

Boockvar believes this could be the start of the end of the AI trade as we know it.

Whether it's completely over or not, investors need to be reassessing how they're planning to play the AI trade going forward, the analysts said.

The Nasdaq Composite dropped 5% on Thursday and fell into correction territory, off by more than 11% from its record high set last month. The iShares Semiconductor ETF dropped 11% on Thursday, its biggest decline since during the Covid breakout in March 2020. The Global X Artificial Intelligence & Technology ETF (AIQ) is down 6% on the day and down 15% over the past month.

"There has been a huge change in the outlook for AI infrastructure buildout," Eric Johnston, head of equities at Cantor Fitzgerald, told CNBC. "The entire economy and the consumer and earnings growth are all levered to that buildout."

The selling Thursday spilled over to non-AI stocks with the Dow Jones Industrial average down more than 1,600 points.

Josh Brown pointed to the 4.8% yield for the 10-year Treasury yield, saying a compelling risk-free rate is causing investors to reconsider taking the risk in these volatile AI stocks.

"You don't need to be at 80% U.S. stocks and have 60% of that exposure be tech," Brown said.

Many investors believe the AI trade isn't over completely, investors just need to get more selective.

"The AI buildout just got harder," said Rob Sechan, Newedge Capital Group Founder & Managing Partner:

"Nobody voted to make compute less scarce," Sechan said, citing Microsoft's Azure cloud business growing 48% year over year in its most recent third quarter. That level of growth suggests its capacity constrained, not demand constrained, he said.

"When new capacity gets harder to build, existing capacity becomes more valuable," Sechan said.

Richard Clode, portfolio manager at Janus Henderson, noted a few bright spots in tech on Thursday despite the highly correlated downward movements in the big tech indices.

"Motorola Solutions had some good results," he said. "We're seeing a little bit of rotation into some of the … internet names...Sony reported some good results, and again they were up strongly overnight."

"So there are some brief pockets of respite from this, but obviously pretty painful across the board today," he said.

Some experts believe the selling was overdone and investors could start nibbling.

This "indiscriminate selling" is a "buying opportunity," said Larry Adam, Raymond James chief investment officer. If investors "take a longer term perspective, which is what we do, I think the valuations at today's levels will tend to prove to be a good buying opportunity," he said, urging investors to look at the fundamentals.

Victoria Greene, chief investment officer of G Squared Private Wealth, said she saw some strong supports around the S&P 500's 200-day moving average and was on the lookout for a market bounce following Thursday's rout.

"Typically, the average return is plus about 180 basis points the next day," she said. "The question we have to ask ourselves: Is this a pullback in an uptrend, which is what I believe? Or is this the end-all, be-all [and] the wheels are coming off and everybody just needs to buy T-bills? I don't think we're there yet."

#2840. Top NFL draft prospect Drew Mestemaker has huge day with Jets GM Darren Mougey watching

Drew Mestemaker continues to make his case to move up draft boards.

It's possible his potential future boss was in the building to see his huge Saturday for Oklahoma State.

The Cowboys quarterback accounted for five touchdowns — four passing and one receiving — as Oklahoma State creamed Kansas State 66-28. And in the building to see the loud performance at Bill Snyder Family Stadium was Jets general manager Darren Mougey, who was spotted talking with Oklahoma State offensive coordinator Sean Brophy before kickoff.

Right now, the Jets, who are conceivably in the market to draft a quarterback in 2027 at 2-6, have the No. 4 overall pick (via the Colts from the Sauce Gardner trade), the sixth pick and the 10th pick from the Cowboys, according to Tankathon.

While a lot of the preseason talk and punditry focused on Texas' Arch Manning, Mestemaker has made the case to be the top quarterback selected in next year's draft. In ESPN draft guru Mel Kiper Jr.'s latest big board, he ranks Mestemaker as the No. 2 overall prospect and top quarterback in 2027.

Pro Football Focus also has him as the No. 1 quarterback taken in the draft.

Mestemaker threw for 4,379 yards last season and has eclipsed the 2,600-yard passing mark this year. He already has six games this season in which he's completed at least three touchdown passes, throwing for five scores twice.

Oklahoma State Cowboys quarterback Drew Mestemaker runs for a touchdown against the Kansas State Wildcats in Manhattan, Kan., Saturday, Nov. 7, 2026. USA TODAY Network via Reuters Connect

The quarterback, though, was pretty amped to catch a 5-yard touchdown in the win Saturday.

"I gotta say catching a touchdown," Mestemaker told TNT Sports when asked about what the most gratifying type of touchdown of his career has been. "That's the first of my career. That was definitely the most fun touchdown of the day."

Whether it's the Jets or another team, Mestemaker could prove to be a game-changer with his arm skills.

#2839. The AI ‘credit card’ is getting costlier as CoreWeave bond yield tops 16%

Debt markets are sending a message to AI data center builders: we're hiking the interest rate on your credit card and may put some new curbs on your credit limit.

That analogy comes courtesy of Jefferies global head of equity strategy, Christopher Wood, who put it in a note on Friday: "The most plausible way the AI capex arms race will end is that the credit card will be taken away."

That isn't happening yet, but credit markets have been spooked by the new political landscape — seeing more hurdles to data centers under Democratic governors in key states, along with a Democratic-controlled Congress.

The poster child for debt stress is neocloud Coreweave. The yield on its 2032 bond with a 9.625% coupon has climbed above 16%. Coreweave's five-year credit default swaps spiked above 1,289 basis points on Thursday, widening above their October peak, according to Deutsche Bank. The CDS eased 8% Friday to around 1,155.

"We've had plenty of warnings from the credit space," said Jim Reid, Deutsche Bank's global head of macro and thematic research, in a note on Friday. Debt and equities are now moving more in sync, he added, "so we'll have to see if credit goes any further."

Investors are demanding more compensation to insure against default among even companies with the strongest balance sheets and credit ratings, including Nvidia, Broadcom, and Amazon.com.

CDS on Nvidia debt is up 76% in the last month. Broadcom's CDS is up 49%, while Amazon's has gained 44%. Their CDS remain low on an absolute basis; Nvidia's spread stood at roughly 139 basis points, compared with about 298 bps for Oracle and roughly 1,100 for CoreWeave. A large increase from a low starting point does not imply the same credit risk.

Still, rising CDS are a sign that markets want more protection against default among even the largest and healthiest debt issuers. And rising CDS imply higher financing costs down the line.

The market is now grappling with the prospect that at least some data center spending will be delayed or scrapped under incoming Democratic governors in Ohio, Texas, and Georgia, the three states with much of the planned capacity.

Moratoriums could impact 45% of planned capacity in the U.S. with a large amount of that in Texas, according to Goldman Sachs. Texas governor-elect Gina Hinojosa has said she's not opposed to data centers but wants a moratorium until the state legislature passes protections for communities.

None of these means AI debt financing is collapsing. Goldman tallies five AI bond deals totaling $67 billion since October 29, pushing total hyperscaler debt issuance this year to $296 billion. Goldman forecasts $440 billion of hyperscaler debt issuance next year, plus an additional $300 billion of project-level issuance across investment-grade and high-yield.

The Trump administration is vowing to support data centers. National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett told CNBC on Friday that he expects data center growth to continue as forecast, despite Democrats' big election wins.

The big question now is how quickly AI spending will generate enough cash for hyperscalers to keep investors comfortable with future capex plans. For the neoclouds, any sign of consolidation or retrenchment in capex could result in fewer or shorter contracts for data center compute or delays in prepayments.

Goldman analysts said hyperscalers collectively reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter as capital spending exceeded operating cash flow.

"Delayed monetization and growth in purchase commitments—which we view as additional and sizable uses of cash, separate from Capex—are the key risks to more debt issuance, subject to market conditions," according to the bank.

Peak AI debt issuance may now be closer at hand. Bank of America analysts estimates that AI-related credit issuance will rise 31% from 2026 to 2027, hitting $685 billion, including public and private debt. They then see it falling 23% to $531 billion in 2028.

As for the risk? "Downside to these forecasts exists from a potential slowdown in AI infrastructure projects on the back of the Blue Wave, which will reduce capex needs and thus debt market funding," BofA said in a note.

-CNBC's Nick Wells and Michael Bloom contributed reporting

#2837. TAKEAWAYS: What stood out from Nets young players in G League opener?

That is the name of the game for the Long Island Nets, who kicked off their season on Friday night against the Greensboro Swarm, the Charlotte Hornets' affiliate. While Long Island ultimately fell 121-111, there was plenty to take away, especially with some of Brooklyn's young players in the mix.

With first-round picks Drake Powell and Ben Saraf having played ample minutes alongside rookie Joshua Jefferson and two-way players Grant Nelson and Tyler Bilodeau, let's take a closer look at their performances from Friday night.

Saraf, now in his second year after being drafted in the first round of the 2025 NBA draft, didn't deliver the shooting performance you'd expect.

The 20-year-old finished with eight points on 3-of-14 shooting, including a rough 1-of-8 outing from beyond the arc. Entering this season, the biggest question surrounding Saraf's game was if he could find consistency from 3-point range.

Despite those struggles, however, he still managed to impact the game as a facilitator. The 6'6" 20-year-old guard dished out seven assists, which led the team. That's what makes him interesting. When his shot isn't falling, he can create chances for his teammates, but that also comes with turnovers, and he committed three in 32 minutes. But a 2.3-to-1 assist ration is seen as a very good number in the NBA.

His passing ability has been known. The next step in his development, though, is developing into at least a respectable scoring threat. The Nets' backcourt in Brooklyn is a logjam, especially with Nolan Traoré's strong play to open the season.

For Saraf to get his chance in the NBA this season, he'll need to be more than a facilitator.

The only other member of the Flatbush 5 who played on Friday was Powell. Like Saraf, the UNC product had a tough night on offense.

Powell tallied just six points on 2-of-6 shooting in 25 minutes, missing all three deep attempts. Despite the rough night, he managed four rebounds, three assists, a steal, and a block.

Similarly to Saraf's game, though, that was nothing new. Powell's defensive potential has been the most promising element of his game, given the length and athleticism he boasts with a 6'6″ frame and a 7-foot wingspan, ideal for a defender. He showed that facet of his profile again vs. Greensboro.

However, his offense remains a work-in-progress, at best. During Summer League, the 22nd pick in 2025 pick struggled mightily with his jump shot, shooting a brutal 1-for-28 from the field, including zero makes on 14 deep attempts.

If he can develop into a reliable catch-and-shoot option while becoming someone who can defend multiple positions at the NBA level, that's a valuable player. Coming away with a legitimate 3-and-D guy at the 22nd overall pick would be a really nice win for the Nets towards the end of the first round.

The issue, though, is that he is now in Year 2 and doesn't look particularly close to becoming that type of player. For someone with his defensive tools, even mild offensive improvements would make a big difference.

Obviously the upside is there, but Powell has to start putting the pieces together.

Perhaps the most exciting part from Friday was simply getting an extended look at Joshua Jefferson. After all, it's been a long time coming.

He was selected with the 28th pick in June's draft, a byproduct of the Julius Randle-for-Nic Claxton trade, and played minimally throughout Summer League as thst trade took until the eve of Summer League to be finalized. He couldn't even practice with his Nets teammates. Then, he missed Brooklyn's preseason due to a lower back injury.

Given all of that, Friday was the first real look that fans got on the Iowa State product, and he didn't disappoint, coming away with 15 points, six rebounds, and three assists, shooting 5-of-9 overall and 3-of-7 from three. Not a bad first impression.

Standing at 6'9" and 240 pounds, Jefferson stands out because he is very well-rounded and brings a little bit of everything to the table, hence his solid box score. There's always a role for someone like that in the NBA, especially with the size that he offers. It's why the term "point forward" was commonly used to describe him throughout the pre-draft process.

Given the abundance of wings up in Brooklyn, it's going to be tough for him to crack the NBA rotation right away, but Friday was a strong showing in the first-round rookie's first legitimate chance.

If there was one thing the Nets knew they were getting when they selected Bilodeau with the 43rd overall pick in June's draft, it was his shooting ability.

Throughout 64 games at UCLA in two seasons, he shot a whopping 43.8% from three-point range. It was on a significant sample size, too, attempting 233 threes as a Bruin. His 46.0% last season was the highest of any player taken in the 2026 Draft.

While it's just one game, that skill was on full display during Long Island's opener. The UCLA product finished with 13 points, four rebounds, two assists, and five steals, while shooting 5-of-10 from the field and 3-of-7 from deep.

Beyond the shooting, though, what jumps off the page is his five steals in 25 minutes. With that outing, he became the first Long Island Net in franchise history, dating back to 2016, to log five steals in a G League debut.

That matters because one of the biggest concerns about Bilodeau's game coming out of college was his defense. So seeing him make such a noticeable impact on that end right away matters…

Speaking with NetsDaily at Long Island's Media Day last week, he emphasized how much he feels his game has already grown since Summer League, adding that defense has been "a big improvement."

"I feel like I've improved so much since Summer League," the No. 43 pick said. "I think a big improvement of mine has been defensively, trusting myself a little more, and getting used to guarding the perimeter. I didn't do much of that last year."

Obviously, Bilodeau's top trait is shooting, and when he gets his opportunity in Brooklyn, it'll most likely be because of that calling card. But at 6'8" and 228 pounds, he certainly has the size to blossom into at least a serviceable defender, and Friday was a step in that direction for him.

Grant Nelson's start to the season on Friday is exactly what he needed to get off on the right foot. The 24-year-old finished with 18 points and 13 rebounds, including six offensive boards while shooting 8-of-16 from the field in 27 minutes.

He missed all five attempts from beyond the arc, which isn't ideal, but it was promising to see strong overall scoring output and activity on the glass as well as a willingness to take the deep shot. The Alabama product also used his size and athleticism on defense, coming away with a block and a steal.

The shots didn't go his way, especially from deep, but that's an area of his game Nelson spent a lot of time developing during his rehabilitation after patellar tendinitis cut his season short last year.

"I think it's extremely important with the direction that the game has been going in," he told us at Media Day. "I mean, I think it's just adding another part to my game, being versatile in that way. Most bigs can shoot now. So being able to do that, I think it will definitely help me out in my career."

At 7'0", Nelson is fascinating because he offers the exact tools that the Nets could potentially need in Brooklyn. With his athleticism, he brings a little bit of everything, but most notably rim protection ability, which Long Island's parent club currently lacks.

Proving that he deserves an NBA chance, though, especially on a crowded roster, starts with taking advantage of his chances with Long Island, which Friday was an example of. Now he must build on that.

Long Island is back in action Saturday night against the Swarm, then faces the College Park Skyhawks, the Hawks affiliate on Tuesday, before their home opener Friday against College Park at the Nassau Coliseum.

#2836. LAFC caps off regular season with loss to Seattle Sounders

The MLS regular season officially came to an end as Saturday marked Decision Day, with LAFC falling 2-1 to Seattle Sounders FC at Lumen Field. The Black & Gold completed their 2026 campaign 12-11-11, finishing as the No. 6 seed in the Western Conference and earning a first-round matchup with St. Louis City SC.

Under interim head coach Xavi Tamarit, the club went 1-2-3 to finish the year, with Saturday's defeat marking the end of a two-game win streak against Seattle. Los Angeles is now 15-8-4 all-time against the Seattle and just 2-5-3 on the road.

LAFC quickly allowed the Sounders to grab momentum in the match after some poor goalkeeping by Cabral Carter in the opening minute. His pass towards Ryan Porteus and Mathieu Choinière was intercepted by Cristian Roldan, who quickly fed Albert Rusnák across the middle for an easy score.

Thirty minutes later, the Sounders doubled their lead. This time around, it was a close-range shot from Sebastian Gomez that deflected off LA's Eddie Segura and over the head of a helpless Carter to make it 2-0. Usual starting goalkeeper Hugo Lloris was held out of action with a back injury.

It marked the third consecutive game that LAFC had fallen down 2-0 to open a match. Once again, the momentum quickly slipped out of their favor and left an uphill battle ahead.

Despite a plethora of shot attempts and possession, the same issues continued to persist. A lack of cohesion, direction and intent left the Black & Gold looking uninspired as they fought to claw their way back into the match. Additionally, Sounders' goalkeeper Andrew Thomas made seven saves, his second-most in a single performance this year.

It was not until the 82nd minute that Segura managed to get LA on the board, cashing in off a well-executed set-piece play. Leo Walta's free kick was first met by the head of Ryan Porteous, who picked up the assist as Segura came flying in to deliver a goal-scoring header of his own.

It was the first goal of the season for the defender, who hadn't found the back of the net since June 2025.

There would be no equalizer for LA as Seattle picked up the three crucial points it needed to sneak into the postseason as the eighth seed. Their 46 points were just one behind LAFC's 47.

Forward Jacob Shaffelburg entered Saturday's defeat in the 72nd minute as a substitute for Armindo Sieb and exited just eight minutes later after taking a hard hit along the sideline. Tamarit said after the match that Shaffelburg was taken to the hospital, speculating that he might have sustained broken ribs.

Meanwhile, forward Denis Bouanga finished his season with 18 goals, falling just two short of becoming the first player in league history to score 20 or more in four consecutive seasons. He finished tied for sixth in goals, scoring the third-most in the Western Conference. Forward Son Heung-min finished tied for fourth in the assist category with 15.

Earning just a single victory since September 12, LAFC heads into the MLS Cup Playoffs in poor form. They'll face a St. Louis group that they split the season series with in 2026, dropping the most recent matchup 2-1 at Energizer Park on May 13. The two sides have never met in the postseason.

#2834. Giants can take commanding NFC East lead by ending Eagles nightmare

PHILADELPHIA — The Giants were headed into their bye week, holding a surprising lead of 1.5 games in the NFC East. The division certainly appeared to be wide open.

"I don't see it wide open at all,'' right tackle Jermaine Eluemunor told The Post at the time. "I think it's ours. I think we just keep doing our thing, week in and week out, we'll see what happens this week with these teams, but we're in first place for a reason, because we earned it, and I think we're truly starting to figure out the team we are now and how good we can be and how great we can be possibly.''

What happened with those teams in Week 8 played out completely how the Giants wanted to see it transpire. The Cowboys blew a lead and lost to the Cardinals in overtime. The Eagles' demise continued with a loss to the Commanders in an inter-division tussle between the two bottom-feeders. As they parted ways for a few days to catch their breath and soothe their aching muscles, the Giants (4-2-1) added to their hold on first place.

Philadelphia Eagles head coach Nick Sirianni looks on during the second quarter against the Washington Commanders in Landover, Md., Sunday, Nov. 1, 2026. Getty Images

This week, they can further distance themselves from the pack. There is Sunday's meeting with the Eagles at Lincoln Financial Field, otherwise known as the House of Horrors for the Giants. There is a quick turnaround for Thursday night's rematch with the Commanders at MetLife Stadium (they tied the first meeting a month ago in Landover, Md.).

"Not to look ahead to the game on Thursday, this is a pivotal part of our season coming up with two division games that can really help us down the road,'' guard Jon Runyan Jr. told The Post.

No, coach John Harbaugh did not print up "2-0'' shirts to encapsulate the task at hand this week, but he did say after beating the Texans in overtime, "The two most important games of the season will be coming up after the bye, which will set the direction for the rest of the season.''

In the past, this encounter was a litmus test for the Giants, and they usually failed it, miserably — highlighting the gap between the two franchises. Now comes quite a juxtaposition.

The Giants are atop the division and the Eagles (2-6) are at the bottom, mired in a six-game losing streak and without their starting quarterback, Jalen Hurts, for the remainder of the season. They take a flier on Joe Milton III in his first NFL start, and there is talk in Philly about rebuilding and regime change — all the discordant noise the Giants heard about their own situation for the past decade.

New York Giants head coach John Harbaugh answers questions from the media during football practice in East Rutherford, N.J., Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. Noah K. Murray for NY Post

The Giants are actually favored in a building where they have not won since 2013, losers of 13 straight at the Linc. That streak has nothing to do with Harbaugh and nothing to do with J.J. McCarthy, looking to build off his impressive Giants starting debut two weeks ago in Houston. McCarthy likely will be without top running back Cam Skattebo (listed as doubtful with a strained hamstring) and, with Tyrone Tracy Jr. on injured reserve, it will be a makeshift backfield for the Giants.

Still, the Giants are finding ways to win, and that can make a team feel good about itself. And that can sometimes open the door for a whiff of complacency to enter.

"Our leaders have talked to the guys about that,'' Harbaugh said. "We're not really anywhere close to that yet. We're fighting for our lives, too. We've got everything to gain, and we know how big this game is. We know who we're going against. We'll be ready.

"In this league, to even think for one second that something's not going to be a challenge or that you might get complacent or that it might not, what would even be on your mind if you thought that? If you're the right kind of team, it's just like, 'Really, you're asking me that?' Shame on us if we would think that way, and we're not thinking that way.''

The Eagles are battered with injuries and the Giants do not care.

"I mean, we got players down as well,'' cornerback Greg Newsome II said. "We lost our starting quarterback early. We lost our best defensive player in Brian Burns early. Obviously, we got Tremaine [Edmunds]. Our job is to fight that complacency, and I think we got the right guys to do it.''

#2833. Quentin Lake apologized to Justin Herbert for "deli meat" and "seeing ghosts" comments

#2831. Warriors rookie Yaxel Lendeborg surprises mom with new house in amazing moment

Golden State Warriors rookie Yaxel Lendeborg has always been vocal about the love for his mother, Yissel Raposo. Together, they shed tears of joy when his name was called at the No. 11 pick in the 2026 NBA Draft to the Warriors in June.

Lendeborg has been vocal about the sacrifices that his mother made for him and one of his goals was to pay her back. He debuted a new YouTube channel showcasing "behind-the-scenes, life outside of basketball and everything in between" where a video released on Friday, Nov. 6 had a surprise reveal of a recently purchased house for his mother.

"On behalf of everybody that came to show up, welcome home," Lendeborg said to Raposo, moving her from New Jersey to California.

Raposo was speechless as happy tears trickled down her face and she took the keys out of a box Lendeborg handed her and she opened the door as they took a tour of her new home.

Lendeborg said it took him 2 to 3 months to finalize the house. To keep his mom from finding out, he and his sister would often disguise phone conversations around visiting Hawaii; instead, they were talking about how to get their mom to her new house without suspicion.

"There is no gesture big enough to properly say thank you for all the love, sacrifice, and support my mom has poured into me but this moment was pretty special and one I'll never forget," Lendeborg said in his video description. "She deserves it all. For me, it's bigger than basketball. It's about making sure the people who believed in me get to enjoy the blessings too."

Lendeborg has always discussed the bond that he and his mother share and how she's directly responsible for his achievements in basketball and finding early success in the NBA.

"I got here because of her," Lendeborg said in June after getting drafted. "She pushed a dream, forced me to go out there and step into the world and become a man. I had no choice but to go out there and do the best I could. So I vowed every day to be a better man, be a better person and just do the best I can for her."

Sharing the moments of success with his mom means everything to Lendeborg because of the sacrifices that she made for him and his sisters, having to work multiple jobs to ensure they have what they need. Raposo also battled Stage 4 appendix cancer, another thing that fuels Lendeborg.

"She put her whole life on hold for my life and I'm super proud of her for fighting so hard and I'm glad we get to share this moment together," he said in a June interview after being drafted.

Lendeborg has been a bright spot for the Warriors on the court, who are on a six-game winning streak as they face the Orlando Magic in a 5:30 p.m. PT (8:30 p.m. ET) tip-off on Saturday, Nov. 7 at Chase Center in San Francisco, California.

The rookie averages 21.0 points, 7.1 rebounds, and 4.1 assists per game through his first 9 games of the 2026–27 NBA season.

#2827. Saints release RB Kendre Miller

The Saints, on the last day before their first game since Week 7, have made a change.

Via Nick Underhill of NewOrleans.football, the Saints have waived running back Kendre Miller.

Miller arrived as a third-round pick (71st overall) out of TCU in 2023. He has 184 career carries for 656 yards, a per-carry average of 3.56 yards. He also has 29 career catches for 217 yards.

The Saints presumably tried to trade Miller but found no takers. He’ll now go to waivers. If he clears waivers, he’ll become a free agent.

Travis Etienne, who arrived in free agency, remains on injured reserve with a hamstring injury. The Saints currently have Alvin Kamara, C.J. Donaldson, and Audric Estimé on the depth chart. Estimé arrived recently on waivers from the Browns.

The 2-5 Saints host the 5-3 Browns on Sunday. The Saints hope to avoid their first 0-4 start at home since 1980 (not including the “home” game in Paris). That year, the Saints went 0-8 at home.

#2826. Game Preview #11: Wolves vs Jazz Game Thread

The Minnesota Timberwolves return home to take on a Utah Jazz team in search of a rhythm. Minnesota is riding a three-game win streak and has found their cadence on defense while getting up the court quickly on offense. This marks the start of eight straight games in Minnesota after a grueling first two and a half weeks of the season. Hopefully, the fans at Target Center can match the Wolves' tempo.

Who: Minnesota Timberwolves (7-3) vs. Utah Jazz (1-8)

The promise of this Jazz team has yet to come to fruition. Utah has won only one game and is bad. They are 29th in offensive rating and 23rd in defensive rating. They are a very young team. The starting veterans they do have are injured.

They are sloppy with the basketball and give it away over 17 times a game on average. Meanwhile, the Wolves boast a top-three defense and are top-10 in generating turnovers.

Ayo Dosunmu has been living by his Jet Life mantra. He is also forcing the most steals of his career. When he pokes the ball away or sees any semblance of open floor, he is breaking the sound barrier on the way to the hoop.

NEW ORLEANS, LA – NOVEMBER 6: Ayo Dosunmu #13 of the Minnesota Timberwolves drives to the basket during the game against the New Orleans Pelicans during the 2026 Emirates NBA Cup on November 6, 2026 at the Smoothie King Center in New Orleans, Louisiana. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this Photograph, user is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. Mandatory Copyright Notice: Copyright 2026 NBAE (Photo by Layne Murdoch Jr./NBAE via Getty Images) | NBAE via Getty Images

The Wolves lost Joan Beringer for the latter half of the game against the New Orleans Pelicans and reportedly suffered a nasal fracture. Trey Lyles did his admirable best to hold down the center spot as Rudy Gobert sat, but he doesn't offer the same chaotic disruption on the boards.

There is potential for Joan to return against the Jazz wearing a protective mask. Should Beringer miss this game, even with the Jazz front court depleted, the Wolves will struggle to get necessary extra possessions.

Anthony Edwards, Donte DiVincenzo, and TJ Shannon Jr. are all still listed as out.

Continuing to lead the attack with all those absences is LaMelo Ball, who has found his shot and has scored 20 or more points in four of the last five games. He has hit shots from deep and at the rim. In the Wolves' most recent bout, he turned around and took Zion Williamson to the cup again and again. He has the handle and the threat to score from anywhere, putting any of the Jazz defenders in compromising positions. His production will be key once again.

The Jazz starting guards Keyonte George and second-overall-pick Darryn Peterson have yet to discover harmony and sound more like H. John Benjamin's Jazz Daredevil album than anything you might find on Bourbon St, the Jazz' original home (and where they should still be if we're being honest).

Nov 4, 2026; New Orleans, Louisiana, USA; Utah Jazz guard Keyonte George (3) acknowledges New Orleans Pelicans center Derik Queen (22) after an overtime game at Smoothie King Center. Mandatory Credit: Matthew Hinton-Imagn Images | IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

Each guard takes turns looking for their own shot when sharing the court, often in the midrange. Seeing this, head coach Will Hardy has turned to splitting up the duo with a mix of starters and bench players.

The Jazz do rebound the ball well, at least on defense. Currently, they are top five in grabbing defensive boards but struggle mightily to recover their own misses. Going against a Wolves team hungry to finish plays or get another chance at a shot, they may find themselves in an early hole.

Minnesota has impressed with their second efforts for offensive rebounds and has managed to maintain the same aggression late into the game when their opponents look tired while the Wolves look ravenous.

In the years since trading Donovan Mitchell to the Cleveland Cavaliers and Rudy Gobert to the Wolves, the Jazz have worked, almost conspiratorially, to lose games and improve their lottery odds. Tanking can lead to selecting a franchise-altering star. It can also lead to a pernicious culture of bad habits, the sort of habits that produce gaudy stats and few wins.

Utah's guards and wings possess the ability to generate their own shot but offer little resistance on the other side of the ball. When a team does puncture their limited-effort defense, they offer even less resistance at the rim.

Both Jazz center options, former Defensive Player of the Year Jaren Jackson Jr. and Jusuf Nurkić, from last year's team are injured. In their stead are journeymen bigs Jaxson Hayes and Mo Bamba.

Speaking of journeymen, Josh Okogie returns to Minnesota as Utah's best point-of-attack defender. Alongside him, second-year forward Ace Bailey has held his own on defense as he waits for one of his point guards to include him on offense. If either Okogie or Ace gets pulled into help, Jonathan Kuminga and Jaden McDaniels will feast on open looks.

Nov 2, 2026; Minneapolis, Minnesota, USA; Milwaukee Bucks forward Jaime Jaquez Jr. (24) works between Minnesota Timberwolves forward Jonathan Kuminga (24) and forward Jaden McDaniels (3) in the fourth quarter at Target Center. Mandatory Credit: Bruce Kluckhohn-Imagn Images | IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

The Timberwolves get out on the fast break and leave the Jazz chasing Minnesota's tempo. The Target Center becomes more raucous as the home team continues to build their lead. Joan Beringer returns with a Phantom of the Opera mask and makes Mo Bamba look like Less Bamba. Isaiah Evans hits two threes in garbage time to balloon the lead back to 20+.

Our blogging buddies are over at SLC Dunk. Be welcoming to our friends with their own kind of funk!

#2825. Joe Burrow can become third quarterback with four or more TD passes in four straight games

Bengals quarterback Joe Burrow had seven touchdown passes in his team’s first four games of the season. In the last three, he has had 13.

With four against the Dolphins, four against the Ravens, and five against the Titans, Burrow has the chance to become only the third quarterback in NFL history to throw four or more touchdown passes in four straight games.

Via NBC Sports research, the two others were Colts quarterback Peyton Manning in 2004 and Dolphins quarterback Dan Marino in 1984. Both won the league MVP award in their seasons of four straight four-or-more touchdown games.

Burrow’s odds of winning the MVP for 2026 are currently at +475, behind only Josh Allen and Brock Purdy.

Burrow currently leads the league with 20 touchdown passes. And the Bengals, after starting 2-2, are now 5-2.

They face the 3-5 Falcons on Sunday morning in Madrid at 9:30 a.m. ET on Sunday.

#2821. How to watch Warriors vs. Magic

The Golden State Warriors return to action Saturday night as they host the Orlando Magic. Tip-off is scheduled for 5:30 PM PT in San Francisco, with the game airing on NBC Sports Bay Area.

Golden State extended its winning streak to six games with Thursday night's 120-100 victory over the Miami Heat. Steph Curry and Draymond Green led the way in a matchup that featured a reunion with former teammate and Warriors legend Klay Thompson. The Curry-Green chemistry was on full display, with Curry scoring a team-high 29 points while Green orchestrated the offense with a season-high 11 assists. As for Thompson, he finished with 11 points on 2-of-7 shooting from the three-point line in his return to the Bay Area.

The Warriors will look to keep their winning streak alive Saturday night against the Magic. While Curry has understandably drawn much of the attention during Golden State's recent surge, the team's bench has also played a key role in its success, providing a consistent scoring boost alongside the starters.

Georges Niang was among the biggest contributors in Thursday's win, finishing with 13 points on an efficient 5-of-8 shooting from the field. His ability to space the floor, make quick decisions, and capitalize on open looks makes him a natural fit in head coach Steve Kerr's offensive system.

And with Golden State beginning to find its rhythm on both ends of the floor, Niang and the rest of the second unit will have another opportunity to make their presence felt against an Orlando team that has struggled out of the gate with a 2-6 record and will be playing without All-Star big man Paolo Banchero.

#2820. 1 thing I loved, 2 things I hated: Michigan Football vs MSU

The Michigan Wolverines lost to Michigan State for the first time since 2021, this time by a final score of 15-6. The Spartans largely owned the game from start to finish, as the final score is not as close as it may appear.

Here is one thing I loved and two things I hated from tonight's game.

It took the Wolverines a little while to get things going offensively, as the first possession resulted in a Tommy Carr interception while the second possession was a three-and-out.

But things clicked on Michigan's third drive of the game, and it was thanks to the power run game. Running back Bryson Kuzdzal took a handoff five yards up the middle on the first play of the drive. After a pass by Carr to Kuzdzal for a first down, Kuzdzal picked up 16 yards up the middle to pick up another first down. Kudzdal then a nine-yard run to pick up another first down for the Wolverines.

It's a shame the drive ended in a missed field goal, but the Wolverines picked up 71 yards on the drive, with 49 of them being on the ground. It was a solid drive that should have resulted in points, but it was a small glimmer of light in an otherwise supermassive black hole.

But other that that drive, I can't say anything else positively about Michigan's offense. It was flat, uninspired and unsuccessful for a majority of the game. The Wolverines compiled a total of 64 yards on their other eight drives. All in all, Michigan threw for 138 yards and ran for 54 yards; Carr threw two interceptions and Kuzdzal lost a fumble. They only scored when the game was already over, and then proceeded to miss the two-point try.

The offense should feel ashamed of this performance. To be shut out against one of the worst defenses in the country at home is absolutely embarrassing.

We've been very complimentary of Jay Hill and the defensive unit this season, but cracks in the armor started to show last weekend against Rutgers. Things weren't all that great against the Spartans on Saturday night either.

Michigan's defense was on the field a lot on Saturday. The defense gave up drives consisting of six (three times), nine, 10 (twice) and 17 plays. The Spartans owned time of possession, taking up 38:07 of game clock. They wore out the Michigan defense early and often, running 75 total plays.

MSU wide receiver Chrishon McCray led the way with six receptions for 92 yards. Bryson Williams also had one explosive reception for 36 yards, while Brandon Tullis led them on the ground with 25 carries for 86 yards.

The offense certainly didn't do the defense any favors tonight, but to give up nearly 300 total yards of offense to that team is unacceptable.

In a game where there's not much to love, I would like to just give a quick shoutout to edge rusher John Henry Daley, who was on another level early on Saturday night.

His first sack happened because he pushed the MSU left tackle into quarterback Alessio Milivojevic. This was one of the highlights of the game for the Wolverines.

Daley had another sack later in the evening. He totaled two sacks, 2.5 tackles for loss, eight total tackles and one forced fumble in this game.

What did you love and hate from the MSU game? Let me know down in the comments below!

#2818. Report: Broncos are seeking a tight end trade, Cardinals rebuffed request for Trey McBride

Denver would like to acquire a veteran tight end before Tuesday’s trade deadline, but that tight end will apparently not be Arizona’s Trey McBride.

The Broncos have checked in with multiple teams about potential tight ends they could trade for, and that included the Cardinals, who told the Broncos they’re not interested in trading McBride, according to ESPN.

McBride is 26 years old and under contract through 2029, so it’s no surprise that the Cardinals see him as part of their future, although there’s never any harm in one GM calling another to see if a player is available. If the Broncos thought there was any chance they could get him, it was smart to ask.

The Broncos’ top tight end, Evan Engram, has just 13 catches for 136 yards this season. Sean Payton would love to get more production out of the tight end position than that. It won’t be a surprise if several more teams with potentially available veteran tight ends hear from the Broncos between now and Tuesday.

#2816. Luka Doncic's injury update: Will Lakers star play against Detroit

Luka Dončić has been the talk of the NBA following a strong start with the Los Angeles Lakers this season.

Dončić and the Lakers are currently in first place in the Western Conference with an 8-2 record and lead the league in points scored, averaging 122 per game through the first 10 games this season.

Dončić has positioned himself as the leading candidate for MVP early this season. He's averaged 40.1 points, 7.8 rebounds and 7.7 assists in nine games played.

The point guard's health will be something to monitor this weekend after appearing on the latest status report.

He was listed as questionable on Saturday, Nov. 7 for the Lakers' game against the Detroit Pistons on Sunday, Nov. 8.

The Pistons have allowed the fewest opponent points per game this season, giving up an average of 101.8 points per game through their first eight games. Detroit is third in the Eastern Conference.

The two-time NBA scoring champion is coming off a 53-point performance in a 124-118 win against the Portland Trail Blazers in NBA Cup action on Friday, Nov. 6.

Dončić is listed as questionable with back tightness for the second time this season.

The six-time All-NBA player missed one game this season due to back tightness the day after playing against Portland in late October.

Dončić does have a history of back injuries during his career. Before this season, he was most recently listed with a back injury in March 2025. He was also listed with back-related injuries during the 2019, 2021 and 2024 seasons.

Dončić enters his third season with the Lakers as the unquestioned star and leader. His accomplishments and achievements are why he was ranked No. 4 on USA TODAY Sports' top 100 player rankings ahead of the 2026-27 season.

MORE: Can Lakers' unquestioned star Luka Dončić step up to be face of NBA?

The Lakers will travel to play the Pistons on Sunday, Nov. 8 at 6 p.m. ET. It will be the first game of a back-to-back and the first of four straight road games for the Lakers. Los Angeles has won its last four games.
